so um i'll briefly to start with background we're conceptualizing
conceptualizing active inference as a kind of cognitive model and what a cognitive model means
is just that it's a formal model that produces behavior of some kind so all of artificial intelligence
et cetera can be conceptualized as cognitive models and this is broader framework where you have some
agent and you have some environment and the environment produces observations to the agent the agent produces
actions that affect the environment and then the agent has a cognitive state called the theta here
that changes over time that could be its beliefs about the environment and um the agent has some cognitive
parameters that could be for example learning rates or its priors that govern how its cognitive states change
and govern how it produces actions and in general cognitive modeling and therefore also active inference has three
kinds of uses that i know of one is theoretical modeling so that's when you run simulations
to see and what a given agent would do given different parameters so there you know the theta here
and you simulate the environment and you want to see what happens and active inference is full of these so
all the simulation work where you're going to show what happens if active inference fish swim together
etc could be considered theoretical models and much of the backbone of the field is built on this
then there's applied cognitive modeling and that's where instead of having the parameters you have the
observations and the actions so you have the behavior for example because you recorded it from a human
in an experiment and you want to reconstruct the behavior that govern the parameters that govern the
behavior you want to find out what was the general model or what was the prior so what was the learning
rate of a given participant that's what's used in computational psychiatry but also much beyond that
and finally one uses artificial intelligence where again you have the parameters but you don't simulate
the environment you have real environment for example you know burning house or whatever and then you want your
your artifact your tool to generate the right kinds of behavior for example a vacuum cleaner that's
supposed to move in some ways and not other ways you can use active inference in all three ways our lab
specializes in applied cognitive modeling so the second of these three and last year we focused quite a lot
on how to use active inference for that but this year we will not because instead we will focus on new
structure of the package and how that makes it more flexible for the kinds of active inference models
you can implement whether you want to use them for theoretical or applied modeling or for artificial
intelligence and i just want to briefly mention that there are many other modeling frameworks than
active inference you know frameworks where you optimize for expected value frameworks where you
run symbolic code operations frameworks where you think them as kind of a dynamic system
in connectionism and later been renamed to deep learning in control theory etc and there's also a
bigger family of Bayesian mind models where you think that the mind uses Bayesian inference so we should
have models that use Bayesian inference to describe the human mind
an active inference which is the kind of variational Bayesian predictive processing model is a type of Bayesian
mind model that does three things so it commits to specifically using variational Bayes which is one way of
approximating Bayesian inference it usually uses message passing schemes to do this and i'll get back to that
and notably it does not only model perception but also action as Bayesian inference just to place us in the
larger field that we live in and you can combine models with these other fields these other frameworks if you
want as well active inference can be combined with them
so as we know Bayesian inference you have some data some observations and you have some parameters or some
hidden states of the environment you have a model m and what you want to find so the general model is
just how the data and the parameters usually co-occur could be just some joint probability distribution it can be quite complex
and as usual what we want is we want to find the parameters that are most likely given the data we get
and we have Bayes theorem and we all know this and crucially we can't calculate the bottom part because
we have to the bottom here to the bottom right and to calculate that you have to integrate across all
possible parameters and if you have parameters that are continuous you have so full continuum for each parameter and if you have many
parameters hidden states that becomes impossible to do that means that usually you approximate Bayesian inference instead
there are generally three methods so on a sampling and i've mentioned these because we can actually use them with active
inference if we wanted to i will show that later so it's worth mentioning that they exist
in sampling you just pick values randomly stochastically with an algorithm that ensures that you're going to
pick a value proportionate an amount of times proportionate to how probable it is in the posterior
and there are many ways to try and do this they can fail they can succeed but the main advantage is that they
can get any shape of posterior in principle the main disadvantage is that they take a long time
and they're not so mathematically beautiful because you don't use equations to get it analytically
another one is simulation based inference where broadly you generate lots of behavior or lots of data
with a model and then you do something to get the posterior out of that and it's one of the classic and the
most used method here is amortization where you simulate data with your model and give them different
parameters and you train a deep neural network to revert that process and then further parameters given the data
and finally in variational inference you minimize a free energy to get the best to get the posterior
notably this is not anything new so it's been used in machine learning and in statistics for a long time
what's new in active inference is that it's used as a model of human cognition in general and it's combined

it's very nice but we want to allow active inference.jl to use any of these options as backend and therefore to allow people to use a wider range of generative models
for a wider range of scenarios but still doing full-on active inference.
So the last part here I will just briefly remind us of two different ways that active inference-like models can be implemented.
One is let's call it planning as inference and that's the one where you just put in your possible actions or policies
as random variables in your model and you do standard Bayesian inference.
Could be variational, could be anything, to find those sections and so this is a screenshot from the tutorial on RxInfer,
very nice tutorial, where they have a discrete pumpdp so you can see this A matrix and B matrix as we know it
and we have a previous state and then they write out that generative model.
So we have a current state which depends on the previous state and the B matrix and a previous action
and we have our current observation which depends on our current state and our A matrix.
So this is specifying the same stuff we actually do in Pymdp normally.
And then we have a planning part where you go through each time step in your imagined future
and you predict forward what would happen.
That's what happens here in this discrete decision from previous state down in the for loop, etc.
And then finally you have your goal prior or your preference prior in the end where you,
in addition to predicting what will happen, you also have a fixed distribution.
Here it's only for the final state.
So here the agent actually only has preferences for the very final state.
So here you run this, you can use a sampler, a variational inference and you get out actions
that are likely to make that goal prior come true.
And given your beliefs about the current state and your beliefs about A and B, etc.
Here there's no parameter learning but you can easily add that, etc.
And it's just to show you could make many changes to this.
It's very flexible, that's the whole point.
What this method does not give us though, is that it does not give us the information gain
that is in the expected free energy in active inference.
So we get the finding actions that give us our goal prior, but we don't give the information gain.
And the action for people, if anyone were at IY, are working at ways to extend this.
So to have sneaky, clever ways of having priors about, for example, the ambiguity that will kind of get
get you the expected free energy anyways, while keeping everything local and using message passing.
You could do this with other things too.
I'll contrast this to, I'll call it modular planning for now, but this is the standard way
that active inference is implemented in active inference.gl, PMDP and in MATLAB.
Where you really have three components to your model.
You have your generative model, the agent's general model of the environment.
Then you have a perceptual part where you take that general model and you form beliefs about the environment
and make predictions about the next time step in the environment.
And this is the coordinate descent variational inference that I described before.
This is the place where we minimize variation.
And then we have a third part where you do your planning.
And here in active inference.gl, etc. you do, for example, a tree search where you go through all possible,
all, sometimes you go through all possible combinations of actions in the future.
Sometimes you do it in a more constrained way in order to find the set of actions that have the lowest
expected free energy.
What's important here is that the planning part is separate from the perception part.
So in cognitive modeling, that's often called a perceptual model and a response model or an action model.
So it subsumes the perception part.
So when you are, especially if you're doing sophisticated inference, you're running your perceptual inference forward in time many times.
So you do your variational inference many times into the future as you plan.
The point here is that they are modular.
So you can have one generative model, say a discrete POMDP with ABC, AB matrices.
And then you can use many kinds of perceptual inference methods with that.
So you could use RxM1 message passing.
You could use RxM1 message passing.
You could use RxM1 message passing.
You could use Turing's sampler with the same generative model.
And similarly, given a generative model and a perceptual process, you can also
with different generative models and or different perceptual processes, you can combine that with the same
planning method.
Because in the planning, you're just saying,
if I do that, what will I think in the future?
If it is doing sophisticated inference.
And you could, that step, what will I think in the future?
That can be done with sampling, with gradient descent-based variational inference, or with message passing.
And it can also work with other kinds of generative models than the discrete POMDP.
I'll note that standard for the planning is to do a tree search.
And tree search will explode if you go very far into the future.
So they get competition very costly.
So you have to find ways to prune, etc.
And there's a whole field of finding good ways to search through that
large space of possible policies efficiently.
But one other option is actually to make the planning stage itself an inference call.
So that itself is a model, which we want to allow in the future.
So you have a generative model, inside of which there is Bayesian inference on a different generative model.
You have a generative model, which is your planning, and that contains the perceptual part.
And that, of course, requires us to nest PPLs.
And that's something we're working on.
But I just want to point out that that's usually what's called metacognition.
So you have a model of your model.
And sophisticated inference, that's exactly what you do.
You have a model of your future beliefs, of your own future beliefs in the future.
And I think that qualifies calling sophisticated inference metacognition.
Given that it's specifically used for planning, one might call it metacognitive planning.
It's just a different word for the same thing, but one that highlights what's happening.
You're planning your own beliefs in the future.
And to do this, you need to run inference within your inference.
And therefore, you need to nest PPLs.

inference about hidden states. So here in the beginning, I'll just use a coordinate ascent variational

algorithm to do learning. So basically means to update the parameters of the generative model
itself. And here by default, we are just using the Dirsch-led capichortical model.
We're going to learn the A matrix. We're going to pass some parameters to it, like the concentration
parameter or the forgetting rate, because there's some volatility in our environment to make the agent a
little more flexible. And then lastly, we specify our action process. So the action process here is
really just a planning algorithm used to evaluate our policies. And this can be standard inference,
this can be sophisticated inference, tree-based methods. By default, in the discrete pom2p module here,
we're using standard inference without graph. And in this case, we're telling it to use the
policy length of one, which we have defined above. And then we're going to use all the three terms
to calculate the expected free energy. So stays information gain, the parameter information gain,
and the utility term. And once we have all these three parts ready, we can just collect them all three
and give it to the AF model constructor. And this will generate the model object for us. And then we'll
just need a couple of more things. We'll need to initialize our environment. So here we have some
pre-made environment. And we're going to tell it that the best arm has the reward probability of 0.9.
And then we're going to switch the contingencies after every 25th trial.
And some random seed. And then before we actually simulate, we're just going to set our initial
observation, our initial actions, initial action, and we're going to simulate for 100 trials. And here
I just have some empty vectors so we can store the beliefs of an agent during simulation for later for
plotting. Now we get to the action perception loop. So the main function here is the active inference
function which takes the model observation and action as arguments and it outputs the probability
distribution or actions. Now because it's a categorical probability distribution, we sample an action
from it. And then we pass it on to the environment which gives us the observation for the next time step.

plot, the first part, the green dots are the reward observations and the red dots are the loss observations.

Thanks a lot, Daniel. Before I start, quick shout out to Peter for the great talk before this. Also,
Peter, best of luck tomorrow with your defense. Exciting time. So yeah, go for it, I would say.
Today's talk is Rx and FUR in action. So my name is Bart. I'm one of the core developers of Rx and FUR.
Also one of the co-founders of lazy dynamics.
So we have been basically at the cradle of Rx and FUR. And I thought the symposium would be an
excellent moment to share our latest developments with the rest of the crowd, get some new inputs,
new opinions. If there are questions, feel free to hop in, right? I think I want to make it very
interactive, interactive as possible. So feel free to interrupt when needed. So what are we going to
talk about today? So for the people who've never heard about Rx and FUR, I want to give a short
intro on what Rx and FUR actually is. I'm aware that most of you already are familiar with this
technology, but just to get everyone on the same line, I want to make sure that everyone has the
same background information there. And from that moment on, I actually want to start building
applications. Some want to show some cool applications that we've been building over the
past couple months, starting with an actual drone demo. We'll also introduce gears.jl, a new package
that we've recently released. We'll talk about our RxInfer MCP server that we've been releasing.
And at the end, we'll have a sneak peek in stuff that we're currently working on. But I won't tell
you what it is. So if you stay long enough for the end of the video, I think you'll get some cool insights.
So let's get started. So what is RxInfer? So in short, RxInfer is an open source library for scalable
probabilistic programming. So what does this mean? Probabilistic programming is scalable.
Well, it has to do with active inference, Bayesian inference. We can do planning, control. Some people
call it explainable AI or better AI. But I think at the core of it is uncertainty quantification. We want
to create programs, algorithms, which are probabilistic in nature, which do not only propagate just values,
but also uncertainty margins, allowing for more robust programming or more robust algorithms.
And what RxInfer does very well is that it scales very well over dimensionality. So compared to
Turing, for example, which is mostly sampling based, RxInfer has a different technology under the hood,
which allows it to scale to problems with a lot more variables and a lot more dimensions.
So how do we do this? So at the core of RxInfer, we have factor graphs.
For the people unacquainted with factor graphs, basically, these are probabilistic graphical models,
which allow us to describe some model of the outside world. This can be the dynamics of a robot.
This can be some assumed dynamics of a signal that we're observing, for example, financial data.
And what we can do is we can create these probabilistic or generative models and decompose
them into smaller terms. Each term is here, for example, one of these nodes in this graph.
And the edges that connect these nodes, these represent variables. So you can think, for example,
that there is a state that progresses over time and each time it goes through some particular transition f.
And that's kind of these equations govern the process on how the state evolves over time.
And we also make observations. So the observations we plug in as these solid black nodes. Basically,
once we observe something, this information will be captured in the hidden state and will be used for,
let's say, making predictions or doing state inference. The benefit of using these factor graphs is that
they are explainable and white box. So we can basically dive into the underlying model
and figure out, okay, what's actually causing the behavior that we're observing.
And because these models are mostly generated by domain experts, by domain knowledge,
we can actually figure out, okay, which piece of this graph, which small box is causing specific
behavior to happen. And it's just like Lego, right? So you can tie together all kinds of different nodes
with all kinds of different edges, which makes it very modular. But all the computations are performed
locally. So although you create a huge model, the computations will be on a very local and small scale,
compared to when you would do, let's say, all the operations at once in the entire large model.
So what we have developed in order to create these factor graphs in code is our domain-specific
language called Graph PPL. And it looks roughly like this. So we introduce in Julia the addModel macro,
and this addModel macro allows us to specify our model. So here we have, for example, a Gaussian state-based model,
where we have observations that we call Y. In this model, we specify two parameters, theta and tau.
And we say each of these parameters will get a gamma prior with rate and shape of one.
The hidden state, we say, okay, it's going to be from some submodel. And that submodel, we can also
define in the model macro. So by doing so, we can kind of create this hierarchy of models, basically nested
submodels. And this submodel is one, let's say, timestep in our Gaussian state-based model.
So we can see here that the next state, so s is a shorter state, will be distributed as a normal,
whose mean is some function over our previous state, and the precision is governed by theta.
And our observations can then be extracted from this state through some function G, where we model
the observation noise with the precision parameter tau. So this is one slice, let's say. And these
function FNGs can be very different depending on the application. It can be drone dynamics, it can be
robotics, modeling financial data. So it can be very diverse. And we can basically use this submodel macro
to expand our Gaussian state-space model, basically putting the different slices after one another.
And this basically allows us to specify this vector growth in a couple of lines very, quite similar
to the domain-specific language that, let's say, turing.cl also uses, but a lot more easier to use
than some of its counterparts, right? There are also a lot of programming languages out there, which should
not offer such a nice DSL. And so I think this is very nice, very great to interact with the tool. Makes it
easier also for outsiders to actually get started building these models without having... So just
some story. So all the way at the start, we had to manually create these nodes and then also manually
these edges, and we had to connect everything together. Yeah, that turned out to be very, very
annoying. So this is basically its evolved version. And in general, we're all very happy that this
language actually supports these features. But basically, they allow us to specify a probabilistic
model in which we want to do the computations, right? The way that we do the computations is through
variational inference. So what variational inference basically does, although most of you are already
familiar with this, is basically it allows us to approximate complex, often multimodal, and skewed
distributions, which distribution that is from the exponential family. So basically, instead of doing
all these exact computations, which are very difficult and tractable, what we do instead is we say, okay,
we're gonna come up and say, okay, we're gonna approximate our result with, let's say, a normal
distribution. And with this normal distribution, we'll try to find the normal distribution whose mean
and variance best approximate the distribution that we actually, the actual true distribution, let's say,
that we can obtain through sampling, but sampling is oftentimes too slow. So here we use variational
inference, basically turning this variation inference problem into an optimization problem, right?
So what we minimize is this variational free energy functional, where we try to find, okay, what Q
minimizes this specific functional for our probabilistic model P. And P is the one that we previously
defined in the vector graph. This looks all complex, but in the end, everything is automated in Rxinfer.
So there's no need to worry about these equations. What's nice about variational inference is that
that scales very well, and it's very efficient in comparison to sampling-based methods, because we
oftentimes have closed-form expressions, and we leverage those to basically prevent having to sample
thousands and thousands of times. And this free energy functional inherently has a trade-off between
complexity and accuracy and accuracy. That's also well-known in the active inference community.
So basically, you can think of it as kind of an optimal approach for actually finding these
posterior values that you're interested in.
Also for this, we have some domain-specific language. Basically, we allow with Rxinfer to give
like an easy interface on changing these approximate distribution skew. So these distributions skew are
the ones that we set. Let's say the simple normal with which we want to approximate a more complex
distribution. And we can also apply different constraints on that. So here in the bottom,
we say, okay, we want our posterior over S, over our hidden state, to be normally distributed, just using very common Julia syntax.
And we can also state as well these different states as we have them evolving over time.
Modeling them all as one big Gaussian is not always desirable, because that grows,
of course, pretty badly with the amount of states. So what we can say is we can say, okay,
we want to actually impose a mean field constraint. So for each individual state in our model,
we create a different queue, which is not correlated with the following one. So by that, we basically can
reduce the computational complexity of this simplifying distribution in order to make inference run even
faster.
So we can do that.
Then putting it all together. So we have vector graphs, we have aggregation of inference.
What can we do with it? Well, RxInfer, as I hinted already on earlier, it doesn't do sampling. It's a bit,
quite a bit faster than sampling based methods. Instead, what we do is message passing.
So basically what that means is we use the local structure of this vector graph. So these nodes,
which are very sparsely connected to each other. And we perform these computations that
basically optimize the free energy. We optimize this free energy on a local scale.
So each node basically tries to optimize or minimize this free energy on a local scale. And that allows
it to do computations, approximations of our posterior distributions. So basically what we can do is we
can compute these posterior distributions, Q, by passing information between the individual nodes.
And this information flow, that's what we refer to as messages. Oftentimes, or almost always,
these messages are distributions themselves. So basically what we are doing is we're propagating
distributions across the vector graph, across the network. What's special about the way that how
RxInfer works on this is that it's fully reactive. So if there is no data coming in, also nothing will
happen because it's lazy by nature. That's also one of the reasons why our company is called Lazy
Dynamics. So we only really do stuff when it actually is very much necessary. And that's also the same
principle that underlies RxInfer. So if there is no data coming in, yeah, the model doesn't have any
information or needs to update its beliefs, right? So it then also doesn't do any computations. So this is
quite adaptive, robust. So also when there are sensor failures, we can adapt to that. Once, let's say,
one of the parts of the model breaks down, like in real world, then we can stop passing messages there.
And because it's reactive, it also allows us to put to every computation in parallel, such that they don't
wait for each other, but they can just pass the messages along each other in parallel, making it
possibly also scalable to new chips that come available on the market.
So RxSperse, for the ones who haven't seen it, it's open source, like the core of RxSperse is fully
there. On GitHub, you can basically find all the information to get you started. We have a bunch of
examples to get started with, ranging from small drones flying around to discrete state space models,
POMDPs, Gaussian processes. And basically, we're expanding this list of examples, like every couple
months, there will be new, like a few new ones. I also recently saw that we have new examples on
icing models, doing some very cool projection techniques for approximating posterior distributions.
So there is a lot, basically, a lot of information in the example. So if you want to get started,
I would definitely recommend to check it. Just go to rxsperse.com and there you'll find the link to
the examples. We also have discussions there, where we are very much open for feedback. If you have
questions, want to know something, just post it there and one of us will definitely reply to you.
Also, if you want to see, like, enhancements to the tool, we very much encourage you to post, like,
your opinion, your wishes there. We are currently looking into the possibility of creating some kind of
logging interface, where we actually are requesting people to also share their ideas, thoughts,
such that we can basically have a look inside rxsper in real time to see what messages are being passed,
and also how the computations actually happen. So basically, just running it in real time,
debugging it in real time. So that's what we are currently requesting ideas for. So if you have them,
please share. And of course, we also have issues. I wish we didn't have the issues page,

So good. That's rxsper. So rxsper, everyone now has kind of an idea of what's happening under the hood.

So we started off with dynamic obstacle avoidance. So we have this drone here in simulation,

What's very cool about this particular demo is that all these spheres that you see flying around,

By making a sharp turn. So just to show everyone what's possible with RxInfer, right? So RxInfer, I think

managed to get these running also in real time. What we've also been working on is a new package. So
RxInfer is fully reactive. And what we intend RxInfer to work with is basically sensors which operate on
different frequencies. We can have, for example, a camera which runs at like 30 or 60 hertz.
Whereas if we have a microphone, it has a sampling rate of 48 kilohertz. IMUs are also somewhere
300, 400 hertz. So we were thinking, okay, this is nice. RxInfer is kind of targeted to solve these
particular problems. But how are we actually modeling them in Julia? Because it's not so easy to
create a particular simulation environment where every sensor runs asynchronously in parallel.
So that's quite complex. That's why we introduced gears.jl. So basically what gears.jl allows you
is it enables fine-grained scheduling for simulations and real-time systems.
So just to give an idea what does it mean? It basically allows us to create simulations of
systems where we have different observations coming from them at different sampling rates. So basically
in the situations where we want to be in full control of when we observe what or when our environment
gives us a new observation to process, that's something that gears.jl has been trying to solve, basically.
So just to give you an idea of how it looks, once you import gears in Julia, you can create a variety of
tasks. So in this case, we can say, for example, every 200 milliseconds, we want to print the following
line that the task is executed at some time step. And then we run our scheduler. So we can say, run the
scheduler for one second. So for the next one second, update. So basically the tasks that we previously
defined, these will actually be executed during this one second duration. And we can make this more
complex, right? So for the drone example, we can say, process IMU data every five milliseconds.
Do this or perform the replanning or update the control sequence every 200 milliseconds or 100 milliseconds.
If processing audio, maybe yeah, process the audio every 10 milliseconds. So we have very fine-grained control
of the actual data sources coming in. And we can run this.
So we can do this. What's very nice about this is that the clock that basically runs these tasks and
basically does the scheduling, we can also replace the machine clock that gets regularly used with some
virtual clock. So basically, if you want to do simulations spanning over, let's say, years, like if
you're modeling, I don't know, planets in their orbits, you want to do something with that,
we can actually also add in a virtual clock, which allows us to speed up or slow down the simulations.
If we want to model specific behavior on like a nanosecond scale, not sure what kind of applications
go there, but I guess there are. Then we can also slow down the virtual clock, such that we're not
modeling on the second level, as our hardware normally would, but on the nanosecond level.
And we have a variety of schedulers to basically create our own simulation environments.
We'll have, we have the intervals that we just saw. So every 200 milliseconds, do this.
We also have something called an ASAP scheduler. So as soon as possible. So as soon as something happens,
perform a task. So this can be used for chaining tasks together and also event-based. So when the
same observation comes in, I always make the example where we have, for example, an API request coming
from another service. That's also something that you can work. And what's nice is that these
tasks, they can run asynchronously. So when you have a lot of different tasks at the same time,
it actually figures that out and you can run them asynchronously in parallel.
So that's just a short introduction of gears.dl. So basically they allow us to create these complex
simulation environments. And in general, I think it allows us to experiment with the core capabilities
capabilities of RxInfer better than just native Julia code. So that was one release that we did a couple weeks ago.
Something that's a bit more recent and then that they're also very excited about is RxMCP.
So what we heard from the community is that although we have this nice domain-specific language,
using RxInfer is not always the easiest, right? So it's still challenging.
So what we wanted to do with this project is basically what we wanted to give the capabilities

Some form of basically
like processing data with uncertainty because that's where it shines. So with RxMCP, I think it has never

So if you have a data set that needs processing, you can throw it in, ask it a question, and it will
basically figure it out. So together with the strength of LLMs, basically converting numbers into some natural
language and using, let's say, the uncertainty processing of RxInfer, you can actually get the best of both words.
We already have integrations now with Mistral, with Cloud, Cursor, OpenAI. You can find the docs as well.

along. To give you an idea of how it works, RxInfer, it starts with some context. So you can think of,
oh, I have this huge data set. I'm going to throw it in one of my favorite LLMs, and I'm going to ask it
to do something. So I'm going to query it. Well, RxInfer will then basically learn from the data that
you have supplied. So it will try to figure out what are underlying relationships between variables.
And it will also predict, so it will give you suggestions, actually. It will compute the
posterior distributions, and those posterior distributions will then hand back over to your
favorite LLM, which can interpret these distributions. So basically, it can give you then recommendations,
based on the query that you provided, and the uncertainty-aware results that RxInfer provided.
So basically, you get the best words, you get the best of both worlds. You get to use the analysis
capabilities of RxInfer, whereas you have your LLM basically explain what it's returned, and why this
is important. Just making the audience of RxInfer a little broader. So I think we're very happy. We're also

up, which stocks are going down. And oftentimes, what the term that they use in these financial markets

to use that investment fund for, let's say, throwing in your pension money, that's something you probably
want to have stocks which are more secure, have the less limited volatility. Whereas if you're like

In a quiet village on the edge of a sprawling forest, there lived a peculiar gardener named Andrey Markov.
Unlike other gardeners who planted their crops in neat, predictable rows,
Andrey saw his garden as a living mathematical puzzle, where each plant had its place not just
for beauty, but for balance and harmony. One misty morning, Andrey stood at the center of his garden.
Andrey carefully selected plants that would support and complement it. He planted onions nearby.
Next, he chose broccoli. Not just because it stood tall and proud, but because it loved calcium-rich soil.
The beetroot, by contrast. On either side, he placed brussels sprouts and cabbage.
Their sturdy stalks forming a protective barrier while their leaves shaded the ground, keeping it cool and moist.
When the planting was done, Andrey stood back and smiled. His beetroot now lay safely within a circle of companions,
each plant playing a role in the dynamic Markov blanket.

processes, P1 and P2 and so on, don't actually happen in brains. They happen only in our

So you say, why does the brain have an analog model of space in it? What use would it be?
It could be very useful because it could help to store a Bayesian model of what's where in space,
integrating what's sensitive. It could be a short term memory persisting that spatial information for short times.
It could be used to classify things to know what things are around the animal. So what things need to be something to be done about them.
It could be used to simulate and plan physical actions. And it could be used to compare the present moment with previous situations.
So if you have the analog model in the brain, it could actually, in engineering terms, be very useful.
There are reasons for it to evolve. And there is a possible physical form for such a model in the brain.
And the pros for the analog model in the brain, which is in an archive paper here, is that there is a wave excitation in the thallus.
And as is well known, a wave excitation, a three-dimensional wave excitation,
can hold 3D spatial information as a 3D Fourier transform in space, just as a hologram does.
A hologram is a physical example of an analog spatial model.
It goes through a Fourier transform, but that's not an extensive decoding operation.
It doesn't require a Fourier transform to get back from the hologram to the real world.
It doesn't require a huge amount of decoding information.
It just requires a scale for the Fourier transform.
Now, why does that fit the data?
How could there be a wave inside the thalamus?
The thalamus, as is well known, is a site of integration for lots of sense data of different modalities.
They all pass through the thalamus.
And the thalamus has a very well-preserved round shape, which is very suitable to hold a wave in three dimensions.
Now, the other piece of evidence that I won't go into is that if you don't have a wave,
that the anatomy of the thalamus, a small round thing in the middle of the brain, doesn't make sense.
It would do much better to disintegrate and move near the cortex.
And another piece of evidence is that lesions of the thalamus tend to interrupt consciousness,
whereas lesions of the cortex, for instance, do not.
And so the hypothesis is that the wave in the thalamus is the source of consciousness.

So how does that relate to the FEP?
Current FEP models look a bit like this, a very crude sketch.
There's a brain, which is a neural computer, and it has an outer Markov blanket.
Inside the brain's encoded information in neural pulse trains.
There's a decoding process, which converts trains into muscular action.
And that goes around to affect the world.
And through the senses, sense data comes in as encoded.
So there's an encoding and there's one particular kind of decoding,
which is decoding spike trains into muscle actions.
But there's no decoding of spike trains into a model of space.
So there is no consciousness in these models.
How do the models have to change to accommodate the evolution?
To accommodate the proposal that there is an analogue model inside the brain,
it looks roughly like this.
The same picture, but made a bit more complicated.
There's an outer Markov blanket between the brain and the external world.
There's an inner Markov blanket between the neurons in the brain and the analogue model in the thalamus.
So there's a lot more encoding and decoding goes on.
Sense data comes in, is encoded as neural spike trains, and is decoded into the analogue model.
And there's a little feedback loop here between the analogue model is encoded as spike trains again,
and it's iterated until the analogue model is a good Bayesian model of the external world.
So there's an encoding and decoding cycle there.
Also for planning actions, there's encoding and decoding cycle here.
So the model is encoded, neurons work out possible planned actions,
and they are decoded to compute the impact of those actions on the world.
So there's more encoding and decoding goes on there.
And the proposal here is that consciousness arises from the analogue model.
So the key thing is that we carry on building FEP models.
We need this extra ingredient in them.
And it's a very necessary extra ingredient because without them,
the FEP cannot account for consciousness.

that we really need to account for.

Next up is a nine minute lightning talk from John Graves.
Here we go.
Many automated systems take data and apply some kind of reasoning called an algorithm to help make a decision.
a decision. The more advanced ones can even plan and execute a sequence of actions to reach a
specific goal. But the most sophisticated systems evaluate counterfactuals. Judea Pearl and Dana
McKenzie called this the ladder of causation in the Book of Why. Seeing is the first rung,
doing is the second rung, imagining is the third rung. The type of questions the system can address
become increasingly complex as we climb this ladder. At the first rung, association,
which is unfortunately about as far as traditional statistics can take us, we can ask how are the
variables related? What if I see x? What then? At the second rung, intervention, there is prediction
about the world as it exists and feedback from actions taken in the world with the possibility
of the difference between the predictions and the outcomes driving a learning process. So doing
enables learning. And here the questions become active. What if I do x? Does the action I take
have the result I expect? At the third rung, counterfactuals, there is a prediction about the world as it
might be or might have been? What could I do? What could I have done? So these second and third level
systems don't just ask what is the answer. They ask what can we expect when we do or imagine doing things?
This is the essence of having a causal world model. The theory behind these systems is presented in the
book Active Inference by Thomas Parr, Giovanni Pizzullo, and Carl Fristin. A learning system operating in the
context of a world model can reason about hypothetical situations and potentially use that information to map
out a sequence of actions which is optimal within the limits of the system's current knowledge. That means
truth is subjective from the system's perspective, but empirically verified. Feedback from the actions taken
can drive learning, expanding the knowledge available to the system, recursively improving future predictions,
and driving more optimal actions. Alternatively, the book Everything is Predictable by Tom Shivers presents the
historical development of Bayesian logic. Shivers makes a number of very well-phrased observations. First,
the great insight of probability theory is that we should look at the possible outcomes from a given
situation, not what has gone before. The probability of an event is the number of ways we know of that that
event can occur divided by the total number of things we know of that could occur. Probability thus gives a
quantitative measure of the things we don't know for certain. It may require a subtle shift in mindset,
but think of certainty as a continuous rather than a discrete binary value. One can represent complete
certainty and zero can represent complete impossibility, but the points in between can reflect levels of
confidence and skepticism with 0.5, representing an equal possibility of true or false, a state of complete
uncertainty. The key insight of the Bayesian method is that you must add any new information that you get to the
information you already have to make each subsequent prediction. Intuitively think of learning by trial and error.
When we learn from the errors, we don't make them again on the next trial. Sampling our statistical probability
is different from inferential probability. What can we predict about a sample, given that what we know or think we know
about the whole hypothesis testing? We don't really want to do that. With inferential probability,
we're really asking what can we conclude about the whole given a sample or samples we've taken?
Bayes showed how starting with an estimate of what is most likely to be true to your best knowledge,
your model, you can make inferences and draw conclusions about what is likely to be true in the world.
Consider, as a practical example, the doctor who looks at a patient's data, particularly their symptoms,
and has to reason to the most likely conclusion, a diagnosis. Diagnosis guides a decision about
treatment, and treatment may or may not result in a cure. Since the world is messy and often ambiguous,
data is never perfect. Systems must deal with uncertainty, changing circumstances, and incomplete
knowledge. This is where genius agents can help. They're designed for decision-making under uncertainty.
The following comes directly from the genius documentation.
The genius agents tackle ambiguity through probabilistic modeling and Bayesian inference.
This is very different from the approach of large language models, which are built on a foundation of
neural networks. Genius agents use a probabilistic graphical model to represent the world, specifically a
directed acyclic graph which links the model variables frequently from cause or prerequisite to effect.
We'll focus on two related types of graph. The first, discrete Bayesian networks are a great starting
point for introducing the core logic and themes behind how these agents reason and make predictions.
Here in the sprinkler example model, the cloudy variable has arrows to both rain and sprinkler, while the wet grass
variable is the endpoint of the arrows coming from rain and sprinkler. The arrows make the graph directed,
and the fact that there are no loops makes the graph acyclic.
The next step is to add probabilities to each variable.
Cloudy is shown as having a 50-50 split. Then rain on the left is shown as having an 80-20 split when it is cloudy, but a
20-80 split when it is not.
Sprinkler on the right is shown as off 9 times out of 10 when it is cloudy, but split 50-50 when it is not.
Finally, wet grass is shown with eight different probabilities, depending on the four states possible
for the two variables sprinkler and rain. For example, in the first column, when the sprinkler is on and there
is no rain, there is a 0.9 chance of wet grass. The notation for expressing these conditional probabilities
uses a vertical bar to denote given. When a probability has multiple dependencies, they are listed separated by commas.
A factor graph replaces the arrows connecting variables with these probability tables, which are then referred to as factors.
This sets up the system to make two types of inference. First, a forward inference, also called conditioning,
where, for example, an observation of either the rain falling or the sprinkler running in green allows for the inference of wet grass in red.
Or the inference can go in reverse, so observations of wet grass with a sprinkler running in green can be used to infer the chance of it being cloudy in red.
The next lesson will cover how the variables, states, and factors come together in Genius.
The HIMO will be used with bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois from bois bois from bois from bois bois from bois bois from bois bois from bois bois

Thank you for joining again, Matt, and to you for presentation.
Thank you so much, Daniel, and glad to be back.
I hope I'm not giving anybody whiplash.
That was a great talk by Anna, and this is kind of a big shift in topic and probably a bit of a provocative title.
So I'm Matt Brown.
Great to be here.
I'm with a company called ThoughtForge, and we're doing something a little unusual.
ThoughtForge began as a research project back in 2007, exploring alternate biologically plausible approaches to machine learning, and we're applying it to adaptive real-time dexterous control for robotics.
Just a little background on myself.
I've been working in AI for almost 25 years now at this point, including time in the defense industry, video games on franchises like Halo, Bioshock, Splinter Cell, stuff like that.
And then about 10 years ago, I was an early pioneer in deep reinforcement learning at a company called Bonsai, which was acquired by Microsoft.
And the last five years, I've been really kind of focused on taking this interesting research technology and applying it to real-world applications.
So just a brief agenda about what I'm going to talk about.
Last year, I gave a talk around the low-level mechanisms about what we're doing and kind of the approach from the bottom up.
And this year, I'm starting from the top down.
I'm talking more about the current motivations and goals for the technology, how it's being applied, and a little bit about the methods.
And I'm trying to keep it to more interesting aspects, more of the philosophical stuff.
So I'm going to start out by talking about robots and dexterity, why it's hard, and why I think a new approach is needed.
Then kind of contrasting current AI approaches with deep learning.
And then kind of just talking briefly about the techniques that we're using and our implementation.
And then I'll show off some videos and applications that we've been working on over the last couple years.
So why are people trying to apply AI to robotics?
And I apologize for anybody in the audience here who's very familiar with robotics because a lot of this is going to be super obvious.
Traditional robots operate in controlled environments doing repeatable things over and over and over again.
And that's kind of what they've been really being good at for the last half century, roughly.
And they haven't really been applied outside of that because the real world is messy, unpredictable, variable.
You know, everyone's aware of these challenges, I think.
What AI is looking to solve or what people are using AI to try to solve are three main things.
Perception, understanding the world, you know, understanding when things are not in their controlled state.
State adaptive manipulation.
People really would like robots to be able to do a lot of the tedious manipulation work that's done by humans around the world on a daily basis.
And then generalization, being able to learn and adapt without having to be explicitly reprogrammed for every situation that they're in.
And at least right now, AI seems to be the only viable path to unlocking robotics in these kinds of challenges.
So let me just make sure I didn't accept this slide.
OK, so these are the kind of cutting edge approaches in AI that are currently being explored and where there's tons of money.
You probably hear about, you know, bunches of companies getting funding to go after AI and robotics.
These are generally the techniques that they're using.
Visual language models basically use LLMs to kind of ground vision and actions through language.
And there's some examples here with RT2, Aurobogen, OpenVLA.
And none of these solutions have yet been proven to solve all the problems or gotten to the point where they can be used in factories reliably or in homes.
I should probably start out by saying that.
And so, you know, each of these have their own challenges.
With visual language action models, they have poor action fidelity, so they're very kind of poor quality motion.
And also, language is not necessarily the best way to understand the physical world.
And they're also very non-deterministic.
And so it's very hard to know with confidence that they're going to be able to accomplish the task reliably.
Diffusion-based control and planning, this is basically using techniques from image generation or video generation, where they use kind of diffusion from noise to generate images.
They're doing the same thing for robotic trajectories, for generating motion.
And sometimes this is combined with visual language action models and with foundational models, so people try to mix and match.
Right now, this is very, very ripe for a lot of experimentation.
The problem with diffusion is that you need really, really high-quality data, and that's really hard to get.
The simulations usually are not good enough, so people are teleoperating their robots.
You'll see a lot of that to generate the data.
But that's also a very tedious way of getting data.
It's also very expensive to actually use in the real world.
And it's brittle to distribution shift, so anything changes, anything about the task changes or the objects change, and then the policy stops functioning well.
And the last one is kind of a broader goal, which is the foundation models for embodied intelligence.
And this is generally people trying to create a chat GPT moment for robotics, where if they get enough data about physical manipulation in the physical world, you'll get to some threshold.
And these AI models will suddenly understand how the physical world works, and then you can kind of use that as a base model to go after individual tasks.
And the problems here are somewhat obvious.
The amount of data that you need is enormous.
The amount of compute that you need is enormous.
And I don't know if I talk about it in the next slide here.
Yeah, I was going to talk about how challenging the data problem is.
Actually, I'll just move on to the next slide.
So these are the main problems with the traditional approaches in AI applying to robotics.
And for the most part, data is the biggest problem.
There's just not enough.
There's not an internet of data, you know, of robotics data to train on the way that chat GPT might have.
And there's a lot of different embodiments, you know, different numbers of actuators, different kinds of actuators, different kinds of sensory input, vision, tactile force, proprioceptive.
And every robot is different.
And so getting enough data that can generalize across all different kinds of robots in the physical world, we just haven't gotten there yet.
And the example I like to give is self-driving cars.
You know, self-driving cars are finally getting to the point where they're usable and they're functional.
But it's taken over a decade of data collection, billions of miles of road driven.
And that's just to handle one use case, driving a car, as opposed to the expectations for robotics are that they're going to be able to do everything, you know, clean your house, work in factories.
And so the mismatch here between the data set sizes and the expectations are really, really quite large.
And there's, you know, same problems that you have with self-driving, with the long tail variability.
It's very hard to simulate reality accurately.
There's just anything can happen.
And similar to self-driving cars, in fact, when one of these models fails in the real world, it has very expensive and often dangerous consequences.
The other problem is compute.
The amount of compute to actually run one of these new age models is absolutely astronomical.
And robotics have real-time constraints.
They're latency sensitive.
So if ChatGPT takes five seconds to give you an answer, that's not a problem.
But if the robot's in the middle of manipulating something and it takes, you know, more than a fraction of a second to make a decision, you probably have broken something or dropped something.
And so the performance constraints are much, much more challenging than in language.
And this is one of the reasons why AI in robotics is so challenging.
This also means that your AI model can't live in the cloud.
That means that your AI has to be small enough to be on a computer right next to the robot because you just can't deal with the latency that you get from working in the crowd.
So the biggest challenges here for AI and robotics still exist in that, you know, they don't really adapt to real-world variability in the way that they need to.
And so what we've been working here at ThoughtForge is kind of a new way of looking at the AI problem in general, using cybernetics as kind of the original body of theory that we're working on.
And obviously there's a lot of connections between cybernetics and active inference.
And I'll go into that a little bit later in a couple of slides, a little bit more thoroughly explaining that relationship.
But, you know, when I started kind of looking at these problems and, in fact, the learning problem, I was very curious about how nature solves these problems.
Because obviously we exist in the world, animals exist in the world.
How do animals kind of deal with these problems?
And they don't do it with huge data sets and huge computers training on that data over a long period of time.
And they don't just train once and then hope that they can generalize.
And in particular, animals don't separate learning from acting.
You know, it's kind of concomitant and it happens at the same time.
And so this is just kind of contrasting here.
The kind of paradigms for what people are looking at with AI currently and what we should be looking at and what we're kind of at ThoughtForge are kind of attacking.
Continuous feedback loops for perception and action simultaneously.
Adaptation at the point of contact.
And learning shaped by physics and, you know, active inference and the free energy principle are kind of foundational principles and theories around that.
And the idea here is to leverage learning in the moment versus learning in the past.
So just to kind of dig into that a little bit deeper, you know, most AI systems today are focused on prediction and are giant prediction systems.
And so the approach we've taken is a huge shift over these old fashioned deep learning approaches.
And I'm terrible at naming.
So deep learning is you can look at most deep learning networks as universal function approximation.
So what you're doing is you're taking huge piles of data and then approximating that into a function, an input output function.
I've called what we're working on inactive synchronization.
I'm terrible at naming.
I guess there's a quote, you know, there's two hard things in computer science, caching validation and naming things.
And we may have mostly solved caching validation, but I haven't really quite figured out naming things.
This is my current favorite name for our approach, inactive synchronization, because I think it's detailed enough and specific enough to be useful.
Other things I've thought about calling it is, but are related, but maybe too generic and don't really give much insight is, you know, generalized synchrony, cybernetic learning, metastable adaptation, things like that.
Homeostatic intelligence or manifold synchronization learning.
These are all accurate, but perhaps not as specific.
And what I mean here by inactive synchronization is that it's a synchronization process with kind of an inactive tint to it.
Inactivism views cognition as a dynamic interaction between environment and organism.
And the world model that's created through this process, through the synchronization process, is co-created through interaction.
Model and control are kind of intertwined.
And so the world model itself is constructed of sensory expectations and motor control percepts, essentially.
And so these are kind of intertwined.
And so this world model is created through interaction, created by the control dynamics themselves, or they emerge from the control dynamics themselves.
So other ways to look at this is universal function approximation, you know, current deep learning systems are retrospective.
You know, they basically take past data and to approximate functions.
And what we're doing is prospective equilibration, basically continuously adjusting to maintain predictive coherence.
And maybe this will make more sense once we get a little bit more into the details.
But traditional approaches are very data dependent, time agnostic, and feed forward, meaning they're input-output functions.
So once you have an input, it computes and generates an output.
And that output is directly related to the input.
And there are ways of making it with recurrent neural networks and things like that where it has some history.
But for the most part, every time there's an output, it's a direct response to an input.
This is very, very different.
These are time-aware, which means input signals are coming in, output signals are going out.
But the output signals, the motor controls, are not directly a computation on the inputs.
They are, you can think of the model both generating, you know, predictions about their sensory experience and motor controls at the same time.
And then where the sensory expectations are incorrect, kind of filter through the network and modify future outputs of the network.
So very different way of looking at the learning problem.
And the goal of what the inactive synchronization here is to build up this model of the world that can kind of predict how the world is going to react to your motor actions.
And then the goal of the motor actions here are really to get the, change the world to become easier to predict or to put you into a preferred state.
And a lot of this aligns very, very well with active inference, obviously.
But this kind of gives kind of a high-level philosophical view of what we're doing, the top-down approach.
So this is just a very, very quick slide to connect the kind of cybernetic approach with active inference.
And obviously these are very closely tied together.
The free energy principle is, you know, I probably don't need to go into it too much.
But it talks about how all living systems act to minimize their free energy, basically pulling from physics a lot of these really great ideas about how the natural world works.
And then active inference is a framework within free energy principle explaining how organisms actively gather information to reduce uncertainty and maintain internal stability.
This is kind of the kind of connective tissue into cybernetics because cybernetics was a field, you know, that's kind of disappeared in recent history, but built upon the same ideas.
Where it provides kind of the theoretical foundation for how feedback and self-regulation leads to this minimization of predictive error and, you know, this kind of self-regulation, self-maintenance process.
Digging a little deeper, our specific techniques are modeled around a cyberneticist known as Ross Ashby.
And he had some really kind of foundational ideas.
He's most known for the good regulator theorem and the law of requisite variety, which are both related to each other in some sense.
And the good regulator theorem, you know, basically says that if one system wants to regulate or control another system, that system has to, you know, have or be a model of that other system.
And the law of requisite variety is somewhat similar.
If you want one system to kind of control another system, it has to be at least as expressive or it has to have at least as much variety as the system is trying to regulate your control.
And he saw homeostasis as kind of the goal of stability, as kind of the universal mechanism for adaptation in the natural world.
And so he built a device in the 1940s called the homeostatic, very, very small, and I'll talk about it in the next slide a little bit more, where he kind of demonstrated adaptation as stability or stability as adaptation.
And it uses feedback between the units to kind of generate a distributed homeostasis through a local property called auto-stability.
So basically he had these nodes that in a network, these were built out of World War II bomb parts.
So he was, this is a pre-digital computer, he was using, you know, doing pure analog computing at this point, using recycled bomb parts.
And each node is basically trying, is performing this property called ultra-stability and when networked together, generate distributed homeostasis amongst the group.
And you can kind of look at the network as a self-model, where each node is trying, this network is trying to predict its neighbor's nodes.
And if all the nodes in the network are doing this, you kind of reach this equilibrium, this homeostatic equilibrium over time through this ultra-stability process.
So just to talk a little bit more about the homeostat, because it is incredibly important to our techniques and what we're doing.
It was the first device kind of capable of adapting itself to the environment, I guess, you know, depending on how you want to look at it.
It exhibited behaviors such as habituation, reinforcement, and learning through its ability to maintain homeostasis.
This, he kind of viewed homeostasis as, you know, the fundamental mechanism for adaptation and complex systems.
And he basically built this device to kind of show it off, kind of what he was thinking.
And it was very kind of primitive at the time.
But, and I think for the most part, people didn't really, really understand what's going on here.
But at the low level, in terms of what he was trying to solve, you know, there's a lot of, a lot of what they're trying to solve with reinforcement learning right now was kind of exemplified in the system.

And so, you know, it's very similar to the perceptron in the sense that you have incoming signals or incoming connections from other nodes with weights.
And, but how we actually update the network is very, very different.
These obviously don't use back propagation.
Instead, they use this ultra stability.
And so another way to contrast deep learning with this kind of homeostatic network is that in deep learning, this back propagation process is a global update.
You have to freeze the network, you know, not use it, do a global update of all the nodes through back propagation.
With ultra stability, the update step is local.
So you don't have to freeze the whole network.
The, the nodes are continuously performing this ultra stability process in parallel.
And so this is, this, this works a lot better for large networks.

So I just like to include this letter in here in my talks just because it's, it's a fun little piece of history.
At the time, Ashby and Turing were, were, I wouldn't call them rivals, but Ashby was very much, you know, pushing for analog computing, this homeostasis way of building computers and computing machines.
And Turing was very much about digital computation.

This is kind of the original analog versus digital argument.
And at the time, you know, analog systems didn't really, you know, complexity theory hadn't been developed.
And so stabilizing analog systems hadn't really been figured out in terms of how you can use negative feedback to make them reliable and things like that.
And so I think they're kind of conflating two things that I think are still being conflated here where computation is not the same as for lack of a better word, cognition or universal adaptation is the different than pure semantic computation.
And, and we've kind of, I feel like this paradigm that we're seeing in AI with, you know, all these different approaches are really just pushing further, further down that semantic computation direction, as opposed to understanding what's actually going on in intelligent systems or complex systems.
