And each node is doing the same thing.
It's basically trying to predict its neighbors as the system as a whole reaches equilibrium.
But you can think of this as kind of a loosely coupled set of dynamical systems.
You know, each one of these is a homeostat, an abstract homeostat.
And, you know, I'm trying to think how much I want to dig into the dirty details on this slide here.
You know, you can kind of imagine that this is still disconnected from an environment.
So we haven't yet, at least at this point in the slide, connect this to an environment with sensors and motors and anything like that.
So really all it's doing is when it's reaching equilibrium is finding a set of positive and negative feedback loops.
You can imagine these connections in the network here forming a large set of overlapping feedback loops.
And depending on what the value of the weight, some of these are positive, some of these are negative.
And what's going on through this local alterability process is that they're searching a state space here of positive and negative feedback loops such that they all are self-balanced.
So all the, you know, once you're reaching equilibrium, it's not a quiet equilibrium in the sense that it's very much active in that all these feedback loops are very perfectly balanced so that they're stable.
And this, you can kind of think of this as a critical state.
And this is really important because it means that the slightest change or the slightest new piece of data can completely destabilize one of these feedback loops and cause cascading changes throughout the network.
And as long as you can kind of restabilize the network quickly, this is great.
That means that one small piece of data can drastically change the dynamics of the model.
And that's what you want in a system.
That's the opposite to backpropagation-based systems where you need a lot of data in order to change the behavior of a model.
Instead, you want one surprising piece of data to drastically change how the model behaves.
That's much more data efficient.
You need a lot less data to get to a useful world model this way.
Let's see if there's anything else that I want to talk about here.
In terms of how this relates to active inference, when it's reaching equilibrium here, what it's trying to do is it's trying to minimize the prediction error of its data.
It's prediction of its neighbors.
And so once you're at equilibrium here, you're basically at zero prediction error and all the networks are successfully predicting each other.
So just at a high level, I have to spend a couple of slides on these next few topics here.
First is how I actually connect these to an environment through essentially motor coupling.
Sensors and motors kind of define that interface between the environment and the model.
Then I'll talk a little bit about embodiment and why this is important.
And in fact, I think to me, the most important aspect of embodiment is the existence of time in the system.
The time awareness of it.
For me, I have a hard time looking at traditional deep learning AI models, even in robotics, where you have a body being embodied because they're considering them to have embodiment because they're time agnostic.
You know, you have these input output functions and they have no concept of being situated in not just in a place, but in a time where their last action that they took is relevant to the current one, for example.
And there are ways to trick this by feeding, you know, especially in reinforcement learning, this is often what you'll do is you'll feed in your previous inference results as inputs to the function.
But I think of that more as a hack.
And then I'll talk about a little bit about synchronization as a learning process itself in case that's not super obvious.
So once you've built a homeostat, even a large one with a large topology, it's still kind of boring.
All it's doing is kind of learning itself and learning to get to equilibrium.
So, you know, one of the first things you have to do is so it's self-synchronizing.
You can kind of imagine it as a self-synchronization process.
So the next thing you have to do is, you know, if you want this to do something useful, you have to connect it to an environment.
And so sensors kind of define how the agent is embedded in the environment.
And the topology itself defines what the adaptive capacity of the homeostatic agent is, you know, what it can do and what it can learn.
You know, obviously, the larger and more complex the network, the more expressive and more complex behaviors you'll get out of it.
And then the sensor definitions, we've kind of, I call them sensors, but maybe they're inputs.
I'm not great at semantics here.
Naming things are hard.
The sensors here are actually really important because they also act as teleodynamic priors.
In terms of getting this to do something useful, you basically can define what the homeostatic set points are in the sensors.
So the sensors aren't just data.
They're not just, you know, pixels coming in, but they have goals implicit with them.
And an example might be instead of having a temperature sensor that's just saying 68 degrees or 70 degrees, instead it's saying, you know, I am two degrees away from optimal or I'm five degrees away from optimal.
So the idea here is they have kind of implicit goals in them.
And those can be seen as like input attracting sets, which you can kind of, from externally, you can modify those in real time to get more interesting behaviors and to kind of change what the model is doing at any point in time.
The motor definitions here are basically just coupling points that go outward.
So they're inside the network.
There's a lot of recurrence, a lot of bidirectional connections.
The sensors are almost by definition input only.
So they're unidirectional and the motors are also unidirectional outward.
And they basically provide affordances to the network for modifying the environment to, you know, and it provides the basis for future sensory predictions.
You can imagine the network here is basically trying to generate motor outputs that help satisfy the input goals, you know.
And so the preferred states of the sensors become the preferred outcomes of the motors.
And so you kind of have this synchronization loop that goes outside of the network.
So the network is doing self-synchronization, and then once you connect it to an environment with sensors and motors, that synchronization manifold basically extends outward, you know, and it basically is performing a kind of homeostasis through the environment, outward through the motors and through the sensors.
You know, the model kind of imagines what it wants and what its preferred state is from the sensors and then generates motor actions to kind of drive it towards that state over time.
So, like I was saying, I think embodiment is a critical feature of what we're building.
And I think the missing piece here is time.
So in these networks here, there's no forward or backwards propagation because there's so much recurrence.
You can't really define a front or a back or a top or a bottom.
You know, signals just propagate in all directions.
The closest thing you can get to is distance to the environment.
So how deep into the network you are and how far away you are from a sensor or a motor.
You know, this is not like the sandwich model, I guess, is what people like to say, where you take input, you compute it, and you generate output.
Instead, you know, information is flowing into the network from the sensors and actions are kind of, you know, flowing outward from the network through the motors.
But once the information hits the network, it just starts bouncing around in all directions depending on the network topology.
You update the network and information flows in all directions.
And through this kind of, when you're reaching equilibrium and you're hitting this state, you know, that's when you can consider that the network is successfully predicting the sensory inputs.
And, you know, whether or not the motors are contributing to that depends on the specifics of the motors and the sensors themselves.
But the expectations of the sensory information here becomes embedded with the motor actions themselves.
So the idea here is the network, while it's generating sensory predictions towards the front of the network, it's also generating, you know, motor connections to the motors.
And these are kind of happening in parallel.
So as it's updating its model to better predict the sensory information, it's also updating the motor actions that are being generated.
And so the kind of the motor actions and the sensory expectations kind of get intertwined in the network here.
And this is why I like to call it the inactive synchronization process, because, you know, these are kind of being co-created as part of the model, the world model that's being directed.
But this this is, you know, if those familiar with predictive processing or predictive control, sorry, perceptual control theory, this will look very kind of familiar in terms of how this actually works.
Finally, I figure I'll talk a little bit about synchronization and think about homeostasis and prediction and where the prediction errors are.
Right. If you if you kind of imagine the drive towards homeostasis as kind of a goal, the distance you are from that equilibrium could be seen as like a prediction error.
And so you're basically trying to drive this prediction error down.
And this becomes more obvious if you actually look at the chaotic attractor definition in terms of the way that you're taking a weighted multiply of your input signals.
And you're trying to get that to be zero. You're trying to you're trying to basically predict that, you know, what your neighbors are doing and a successful prediction will result in a local prediction error that is near zero.
And when when the network and when this happens, you can kind of think of it as a synchronization process when when you're you can when you can act when you can successfully predict your neighbors, you're kind of synchronized with them.
You know, you know what they're going to do before they do it. And so that's how you can have the sensory expectations meet the sensory inputs through a synchronization process.
And synchronization is kind of magical. Once once you have two systems that have synchronized or let's say you have multiple systems synchronized and you can imagine each node in here synchronized to its neighbors.
And so you kind of have this synchronization over a distance.
that's a way for two systems that are not in direct communication to coordinate their behaviors.
And so, you know, you kind of get very interesting collective behavior out of these networks, collective here of the nodes through the synchronization process where the each individual node behavior is going to be
consistent with or
or
or
coordinated with distant nodes in the network.
And so this is a very important aspect of of how these functions work, because if you had to if if two distant nodes that are representing different aspects of the environment had to be in direct communication all the time in order to maintain their their correct modeling of the world,
this whole system would become incredibly computation expensive and impossible and intractable.
So, so the synchronization process is very important.
I think I talked about previously how this is maintained at a critical state.
And so the smallest prediction error that's coming in through the sensor can have cascading effects through the network that cause very, very fast destabilization.
And so the trick here is to synchronize faster than the environment can destabilize it.
So if you're updating your network too slowly and the environment is changing very, very quickly, you're going to be constantly in a state of chaos.
But if you can update your network much, much faster than the the changes that are being experienced in the environment, you can imagine that this network is able to re synchronize and re re stabilize continuously over time faster than the environment can.
And this, this goes back to kind of Ashby's law of requisite variety.
And the idea here is that the variety that's being generated is very much time dependent and depends on the rate of change of the environment versus the rate of change of the network.
But the trick to this is ultra stability is very, very efficient, and then we can run it really, really quickly.
And in most of our robotic applications, for example, these networks are operating usually around the update state, the update rate of these individual nodes.
And so for robotic applications, usually between five kilohertz and 50 kilohertz.
So we do this very, very quickly.
And depending on the application and what's being done, that's generally fast enough to maintain the synchronization manifold between the model and the environment.
You know, it's a it's a prediction error minimizing synchronization manifold.
But if you if you run the network that fast, you're able to basically maintain the synchronization manifold over time.
And that's how we kind of get useful behavior.

But I think, I think if the money keeps going in that direction, that's what you're going to see.

There are a lot of startups that are just hiring people to do a task over and over and over again.

Hello, everybody.
Uh, thanks for having me in this wonderful symposium.
My name is Maria Luisa Iannaca, but you can call me Maria.
I am a PhD candidate in philosophy at the university of São Paulo and the university of Porto.
Uh, well, my talk will be, I think a bit different from, from the rest because, um, I come from philosophy and I will try to discuss the ambiguous status of attention in, uh, predictive models of cognition.
So, um, the content, uh, will be divided into like a brief description of precision modulation and salience, followed by a description of attention across disciplines.
And then I will finish with this discussion that I've been, uh, labeling as, uh, having an ambiguous status.
And, you know, as I am a philosopher, again, this is a philosophical analysis.
And so my focus will not be, uh, on any specific models or any specific formalisms, but on the general framework of both hierarchical predictive coding or predictive processing and active inference, which I believe you all know very well.
Okay.
Okay.
So, uh, attention in these two frameworks, uh, is usually related to two things, precision modulation and salience.
Precision modulation or, you know, precision weighting or precision optimization is, uh, formally the expected inverse variance of prediction errors.
In other words, uh, in the predictive brain, the precision modulation happens within second order predictions that control the confidence of the first order predictions.
So, if the brain is highly confident in a prediction, the modulation will reduce the gain on error signals, choosing to trust its prior beliefs over the unreliable new data.
However, if the context is uncertain or noisy, uh, an error will be given, uh, significant weight, you know, prompting a major update to its internal model.
And it is believed, uh, to be implemented via lateral or self-inhibition in the error units with the biological substrates being, uh, like inhibitory synapses, neuromodulatory adjustments, and also fast gamma synchrony.
And attending here, uh, would be the modulation of, uh, uh, the synaptic gain afforded to top-down predictions and bottom-up, uh, prediction errors based on the precision they are estimated to have.
And this is pretty much how the endogenous and exogenous attention would work.
Salience, on the other hand, has to do with action selection.
So, salience has been, uh, characterized, uh, as intrinsic motivation and denotes actions promising maximum information ratio gain.
Uh, that is, uh, highly unpredictable and yet interpretable observations after which sampling is suppressed in a manner akin to inhibition of return.
And in neurobiology, this would be tied to action planing, uh, specifically, uh, in vision, we would say, uh, the action of basal ganglia, uh, where the salience ranks potential saccadic targets and thus corresponds to competing saccade, uh, evaluations.
So, attention then comprises actions selected to elicit information that cannot be referred passively.
And salience indexes the epistemic value of such actions as quantified by, uh, expected free energy.
And, of course, uh, the predictive attention has been around for a while across, you know, various disciplines.
And here is just an example of three tasks that were simulated using predictive models in visual neuroscience.
But, you know, uh, there are tons, uh, of different other examples in the literature.
So, this first, uh, on your left is the famous Posner queuing task, which revolutionized, uh, the neuroscience of attention.
And it was successfully simulated by Feldman and Freeston in 2010.
And this task basically, uh, makes participants fixate, uh, centrally while cues indicate where a target might appear.
If the cues are valid, it will speed up the reaction times when the target appears at the queued location.
If the cue is invalid, slow responses will happen, uh, when the target appears as well.
And it's, you know, uh, uh, uh, people say that it's reviews how attention can be, uh, covertly shifted without eye movements.
And the example in the middle is an active inference model of selective attention developed by Mirza and colleagues in 2019.
Uh, they focused on contextual epistemic foreaging.
That is how agents seek out task relevant information while ignoring irrelevant stimuli.
And for that, they simulated typical attentional behaviors like this one in Yavuz task, uh, where you basically, uh, have a picture and you, and you do, and you have to look to this picture.
And then, uh, your, uh, your saccades will be, uh, recorded.
And so on the left, uh, side of the figure, uh, there is the human saccades.
And on the right side, there, there are the simulated ones.
And they framed attention, uh, not just as the, uh, reactive process, but as a proactive part of planing that will be tightly linked to the saccadic eye movements and also believe updating.
And the third example on the right is more focused on the movements of the eye itself and also the neuromodulation that happens.
Uh, and this paper was done by Paul Freeston and they used an active inference model to simulate how different, uh, neuromodulators, specifically the cholinergic and the gabagic modulators affect, uh, ocular motor behavior during, uh, delayed saccade task.
And this task is about a cue that indicates a target location followed by a delayed period requiring fencing session.
And then, uh, saccades to the remembered location.
And so by manipulating precision parameters associated with each neuromodulator, they, uh, were able to model how pharmacological interventions could alter both the decision to initiate a saccade and the trajectory of the eye.
And so predictive models have also been used in computational psychiatry.
That is, uh, a new and progressively important area that aims to, you know, understand the psychiatric disorders using both real data and simulations and models.
And the majority of these studies work with predictive coding schemes instead of active inference.
So they kind of focus on the precision modulation parts instead of salience.
And I'm not going, uh, in details about these papers that I put, uh, on my slide, but, you know, I, I left them here just for you in case you want to, uh, check, uh, later and read if you want.
But the idea that I wanted to say here is this many, if not all the psychiatric disorders in the predictive context happen because of the malfunctioning of precision modulation.
And even though the malfunctioning is not the only or the main reason as everything, you know, about our psyche happens because of multiple things, it is a current and a core reason.
And this means that if we understand attention as precision modulation, then all psychiatric disorders could be, in fact, attentional disorders, at least in principle.
So can we, you know, really say that attention is precision modulation?
Well, attention, like, uh, any other phenomenon of the mind has been studied since forever, uh, across cultures and fields.
But one definition that was kind of foundational to, uh, the science of attention was the one made by William James.
That is, uh, the taking possession by the mind in a clear and vivid form of one out of what seems several simultaneously possible objects or trains of thought.
Focalization and concentration of consciousness are of its essence.
And it implies withdrawal from some things in order to deal effectively with others.
And is a condition which has a real opposite in the confused, dazed and scattered brain state.
This is like the classical definition.
But more recently, uh, neuroscientists Sabine Kastner and Kianobri coined a more scientific or more operationalizationable, uh, definition.
That is the prioritization of processing information that is relevant to current task goals.
And a couple of years ago, philosopher Wayne Wu made an even shorter description and operational, operationalizable definition of attention.

Now, how does this link to standard financial theory?
So when we take an introductory corporate finance or investment management course, the first pricing model that we usually learn is called the CAPM, the capital asset pricing model.
So the CAPM right here is essentially a regression model, a one-factor regression model that essentially says all expected returns on a security, let's say on an equity, on a stock, depend on just one factor,
which is the market risk premium.
So essentially, the model says you are strictly and only being compensated by the amount of market risk that you are taking.
So we can decompose the CAPM into two types of essential risks in financial investments.
One is called systematic risk.
So that systematic risk is pretty much what I just told you, the market risk.
How much market risk are you taking?
And that's the only risk, according to this financial theory, that you get compensated for.
Whereas the other term is that idiosyncratic risk.
So that idiosyncratic risk, we can refer to it as the firmest specific risk.
So for example, if I'm investing in a restaurant, I am exposed to specific risks that the restaurant has,
like having a fire or then employees don't show up or a flood or whatever.
But that's not systematic risk.
When we speak about systematic risk, we refer to it as general market risks, like inflation risks, like interest rate risk.
So COVID as well, those are systematic risks that we cannot control in any way.
So we cannot diversify it away in any way in standard portfolio management theory.

And we are portraying here the last 10 years of performance of her most popular fund, the ARC Innovation ETF.
That pretty much is an investment fund that gives exposure to investors to the major trends, again, in innovation.
Now, as you can see, the fund performed extraordinarily well since 2015 up to 2021.
So it went up 700% in five to six years, 700%.
So at that time, Katie Wood was appearing in TV all of the time.
She was claimed to be the next Warren Buffett.
And her fund draws, I mean, drawed investments for more than $20 billion.
So it became extremely popular.
Now, as of now, I mean, after that peak, it went down 80% in its lowest point.
And even after almost six years, people who were most of the investors who entered into the peak have not recovered even their initial capital.
Now, I mean, why we like it is because this type of popular, trendy, momentum-chasing type of investment is precisely the type of asset that allows us to measure regime shift.
Because almost visually, we can see that that would be a bull, a bear regime shift, and then more or less a flat regime shift.
So we are using ARC as an example.
And how we're going to train our active inference agent, taking almost like a standard machine learning type of training methodology, where we're using 70% of the historical information.
And we're performing a backtest on the last three years from 2022 to 2025 to see how our agent would perform.
Now, what's the advantage? Because one may say, why not use just deep learning and NLM?
And the advantage is that we don't require retraining to just keep following the performance of the ETF.
Because by definition, this type of active inference implementation is adaptive.
It's adaptive learning. It's full online based on learning.
So that's a completely different way to do machine learning.
And I would claim it's a much more efficient way.
And also, I think for financial applications, this adaptivity makes you much more prepared to adapt to changing market circumstances,
especially with this type of volatile assets, which are one of many in the financial markets.
Now, just a brief theoretical bridge as well with standard financial and economic theory.
Using the Helmholtz decomposition, which is a standard part of the active inference and free energy principle,
we know from Helmholtz that we can decompose any vector field into two components or two parameters.
One is the gradient, we can say, almost a deterministic component.
And the other is the selenoidal flow, which is the cycle that is never changing into the model.
Now, how can we build a link between this theory behind active inference and financial economics?
One model, which is one of the most important models in valuation, is the Black-Scholes-Merton option pricing formula.
So this model was created in the 70s and very quickly became the standard to price financial insurance, financial options in markets in general.
But originally, the model was created to value corporate liabilities, actually.
So another extension of the Black-Scholes model is what we call the real options Black-Scholes-Merton model,
which can be actually applied to value private projects, private equity projects,
and mostly innovative projects where there's a contingent aspect into that type of investment,
where there's huge changes.
For example, like all of the innovation trends embedded in a fund like ARK.
Now, what I mean, the Black-Scholes-Merton option pricing formula defines that stock prices move
as a geometric Brownian motion, where you have a drift, the first term here, defined by mu,
and a stochastic component where you have the volatility here and the Wiener process,
which does the stochastic part.
But what is the probability evolution of this model?

So there's no selenoidal flow.
I mean, put into economic terms or into physical terms in this case,

So if it always reaches equilibrium, there will be no exploration,

So what we're claiming here is that this agentic active inference framing
is a non-equilibrium dynamics applied to financial markets,
and it can be applied to other type of markets,
where we know that even though at certain very quick points in time,

And the reason why, again, is because standard equilibrium economics
assumed that agents were sort of statics, sort of robots.

And in this sense, we say that the synergetics theory,

I'm Viet.
I'm a PhD candidate at Rochester Institute of Technology.
I'm currently working with Dr. Ovia in the Neuroadaptive Computing Lab.
And yeah, today I will be discussing our work, active neural robotics, deep prior preference
learning guided by our learnable goals.
But first of all, I want to talk a little bit about introductions and motivations.
Yeah, so we can see nowadays there's a lot of applications of robotics in several different fields.
Right?
So for example, robotics can be applied in industrial manufacturing.
For example, speeding up assembly processes in factory HIM will be used with a large large
HIM will be used with a large large large large large large large large large large large large
large large large large large large large large large large large large large large large large
large large large large large large large large large large large large large large large large
But first of all, I want to talk a little bit about introductions and motivations.
So we can see nowadays there's a lot of applications of robotics in several different fields.
So for example, robotics can be applied in industrial manufacturing, for example, speeding
up assembly processes in factories can be applied in healthcare, can be applied in farming,
agriculture, and constructions.
It can also be further applied in locomotions, for example, self-driving car and space exploration.
But there's several problems in this where most robots cannot operate and learn autonomously.
And then they actually cannot adapt to new environments as well.
Generally, training a robot learning algorithms actually costs a lot of amount of resource for
data collections.
That's a lot of money, right?
And then the safety measures of robots are often not learned.
So it cannot be flexible enough to work safely around humans.
So to overcome these barriers, research has actually investigated different ways to tackle
this.
For example, one way to tackle this is to investigate neuro-robotics, which is a subfield of robot
learning.
Basically, it's modeled a robot's learning process, very similar to that of a human.
So a biologically inspired auto-modus learning system often investigates human perception, memory,
attention, sensory motor control, and so on.
So this neuro-robotics field also features robot learning from real-world interactions or
online learning.
So with online learning, a robot can adapt to new environments with less data collection
costs and then can be learned to be set around humans.
So first of all, we need to, in order to do like neuro-robotics, we need to investigate
cognitive architecture.
So the cognitive architecture is very much studied in the active inference process theory.
So basically, it investigates different modules inside the brain that can be used to learn
a perception system.
So first of all, there's the generative model, which is basically the perception system itself.
So it's basically trying to, through the sensors, making observations in the environments and
then learn the actual interactions between the environment and the agent.
The second one is the motor, which is the action controller.
It's actual, the action will be taken by the agent and then executed inside the environment
to return a new observation.
And then there's like the memory module, which stores the interactions between the agent and
the environment.
So that memory is kind of memories will be stored in here in order for future use of like training
or, you know, just maybe remembering of knowledge and knowledge purifications, for example, or distillations.
So basically, the prior preference distributions trying to predict the desired representation or the desired goal that the agents want to achieve in the future.
So generally, in active inference, a prior preference distribution is provided in advance.
But in our work, we are trying to have like a separate neural network to predict the prior preference in the future dynamically.
So the last one is sensors, right?
Like for example, the robot has like eyes and arms and so on.
So before going to the main topic, I want to touch on a little bit about deep active inference and the background around it.
So basically, the interactions between the agent and the environment can be described as the interactions between the generative process and the generative model.
So first of all, is the generative model process.
Basically, it's the real world environment itself, right?
And inside the real world environment, there's like a hidden state.
So this is like the true probability distributions of the environment and we cannot actually see it.
And in here, we can see that there's an arrow between the true state and the observations, which means that the generative process actually generates the observation that can be observed, observable.
So the agents can actually see this observation.
And this, when compared to like, it is very similar to a partially observable Markov decision process that is often formulated in the reinforcement learning literature.
So now when we have the generative process, we have the generative model, which is the actual agent itself.
So the agents also have a state distributions here.
So it is actually the agents best approximations of this generative process.
Basically, we can call it like a dynamic states or a agents belief state or a world model states, however you want to call it.
So this distribution will be best estimates of the hidden states inside the environments.
So the hidden states here can be like the world dynamics, for example, or the physics or the logic of the world, for example, something like that.
And in active inference process theory, the agents basically continuously trying to make the observation through time and also continuously modify its belief after making the observation.
So how do we further formulate this?
First of all, we have the agent states here.
It is like the initial belief or representation or the world dynamic states.
It's going to be called the prior over states, which is P of s.
And then we also have P of O, which is the model evidence, which is the observation distribution.
And then in active inference, we are trying to have the agents both observing and predicting.
So first of all, we predict the observations, which is basically the likelihood of observations.
It is the agent's best estimate of the observation distribution given the prior or the posterior information that it has.
And then finally, the P of s given O is posterior function.
Basically, it's the agent's best estimate of the state after making the observation.
So it's going to be after making the observations, the agents actually making or changing the posterior belief.
So now we have all these definitions of all these functions.
How do we learn this generative model?
So we have to learn, first of all, the likelihood functions and then the posterior approximation.
And then we have to best estimate the model evidence distribution here as well.
And then we estimate the posterior and then the prior.
So how do we learn this?
So active inference process theory is proposed that we are trying to minimize the variation of free energy or the marginal free energy.
So in order to do that, we first have to predict the likelihood of the observation or the modal evidence.
So in deep active inference generally, this is actually called the decoder and it is often parameterized by your neural network.
Next is the posterior functions.
So at the same time when we are trying to predict the observation, we also have to make an observation.
And then we produce the approximate posterior at the current time step.
So in deep learning literature, this is often called an encoder.
And it is also often parameterized by a neural network as well.
So in here, active inference state stats, we have to try to continuously correcting the belief after making new observations.
So we are trying to minimize the belief after we have made observations and the belief states prior to making observations.
So we are trying to minimize the surprise between those two kind of distributions.
That also means that we are trying to minimize the posterior and the prior distribution.
That also means that we are trying to minimize the care divergence between the posterior Q of S and the prior Q of S.
So this is also means that we are trying to correct the belief after making any observations.
And then next is in parallel to minimizing the uncertainty between the posterior and the prior.
We also have to maximize the prediction accuracy of the observation as well.
So in this case, we are trying to maximize the similarity between the true observations and the predicted observations.
And this is also called maximizing modal evidence or maximizing observation prediction accuracy.
And we do this by minimizing negative log likelihood of the predictions.
So that is for like one step.
Right. And now we want to extend it to multiple steps.
And then we have to integrate actions here as well.
And so at the time step T, there is several different, there's hidden states, right, that produce the O of T, which is the observation that the agent can see.
And then the S of T is like the actual initial, it can be prior or posterior of the agent.
And then there's the actions of the agent that the agents plan to take.
And so when the agents take the actions in the environment, it is executed and it's produced a new state, a new hidden state that we cannot see.
And then produce a new observations, right.
At the same time, the agents can estimate the next state given the previous state, S T and the action S T.
It can estimate the next state S T plus one here.
And then we do the same process, right.
So S T plus one is actually the prior of the next state.
And we can do the same process by predicting the observations of the next state.
And at the same time, we observe the observation of the next state and then encode it to an approximate posterior.
And then we minimize the variational free energy by minimizing the KL divergence between the posterior and the prior,
and then maximizing the prediction accuracy by minimizing negative log likelihood of the prediction, O T hat plus one.
So here's the formula for the variational or marginal free energy.
You can see that in here, there's like a complexity term where it's minimizing the KL divergence between the Q of S given O
and P of S given S T plus one minus A T plus one and A T plus one.
It's basically the previous state and previous actions that's caused the current state, right.
Leads to the current states in the current prior.
And then this complexity term is like measuring the amount of surprise that we want to minimize.
And then finally, we have like the accuracy term where we are trying to maximize the prediction accuracy of the observation.
And so we're trying to minimize the negative log likelihood of the prediction observation distribution.
So now when we have done the learning for the world model or the generative model, we have to learn the policy as well.
Right. So we have to know a way to like decide what kind of actions should be executed in order to optimize or solving the task.
So there's so many actions that can be taken in one single time step.
And each of those actions can lead to like different possibilities.
So the agents can like estimate like each possibilities based on like previous actions in the current belief.
And so on. Right. So there's like as the time steps or the horizons spans out the number of possibilities of the belief states actually goes up a lot.
Right. So how do we choose the optimal trajectories of actions that we want to take?
So that's exactly like this.
That brings us to the expected free energy.
Right. So we are trying to say that whatever kind of trajectories in the future.
That yields the lowest expected free energy.
Then we choose that as a optimal trajectories of actions that we want to choose.
And then we learn the policy on that.
That's exactly like this.
The expected free energy is eventually decomposed into an epistemic term and instrumental terms.
So in this topic, I only focus on the instrumental signals.

So basically, we're trying to keep the RSSM the same or fixed when we are trying to learn the CRSPP or the prior preference model.
So the point or the goal of learning the prior preference model is trying to have that model predicting the optimal trajectories, right?
The optimal trajectories of the state in the future such that those state trajectories represent the goal or the preferred state or however you want to call it.
So in order to do that, we have to collapse the state of the prior preference model to the state of the RSSM whenever the trajectory is successful.
And in contrast, we have to pull away or push away from the RSSM state when the trajectory is not successful.
So in order to do that, we have to introduce a term called role or prior preference rate.
So we're literally saying whenever the trajectory is successful, the role is positive.
This positive term will collapse all these two states together, right?
And then whenever the role of prior preference rate is negative, then these two states are pushed away from each other.
So when we keep the state of the RSSM fixed or stop the gradients of the RSSM, then only the state that gets pushed and pulled is the state of the prior preference model down here.
So in the end, the CRSPP or the prior preference model will learn to only predicting the optimal state of the RSSM without predicting the failing states.
So you can see that in here there's a little bit difference, right?
So the RSSM is not conditioned or the RSSM is conditioned on the actions, right?

So we'll begin with motivation and background,
and then we'll go into the four main corners of our project,
then implementation, how we did it,
and then how we evaluated how well the model did,
and then just the last conclusions and whatnot.
So first, let's start with motivation and background.
So the first challenge that motivated this project
is defining action spaces.
When you consider action spaces, you
have to look at continuous spaces and discrete spaces.
Each space has its own advantages,
but continuous spaces.
You have to read about computational efficiency
and suboptimal solutions.
But defining discrete spaces that are super flexible
is really tough, especially when you consider
that you have infinitely possible discrete actions,
but you need to define a space somehow.
Our next challenge is abstractly representing the environment.
So for planning, an agent needs to understand
what its environment actually is.
For humans, we can just say there's a tree,
or that's a computer, but an agent can't just do that.
So how do we turn these raw sensor data that we can give an agent
into meaningful ideas?
There are some issues with deep learning,
like it doesn't have an internal world model,
and it learns correlations.
And while deep learning scales by avoiding the structure,
active inference actually builds up a structure from the ground up.
And we want to combine deep learning scalability with active inference's
structured understanding for a good environment representation.
The last challenge that motivated this project is considering computational efficiency.
In robotics, offloading competition to GPUs or cloud servers is often not possible when you
consider issues like latency and bandwidth limits.
This requires us to consider edge processing or on-device processing.
Both deep learning and active inference are computationally intensive for various reasons.
And our goal with this project is to maintain the active inference
framework's interpretability and adaptability while maintaining real-time inference and control on edge devices.
To address these challenges, we combine four main concepts.
First, hierarchical collective inference.
As from the diagram on the left, the brain works hierarchically with higher level prediction and planning
and lower level perception modules.
Each layer updates beliefs to reduce prediction error.
Hierarchical active inference models this top-down, bottom-up like blue
by minimizing uncertainty at each level to guide adaptive behavior.
Information from lower levels is compressed into higher levels.
And you still retain these key concepts, which is the main thing which will address a lot of our challenges.
And it also allows, in practical use, computation can be distributed among multiple levels.
The next concept is a foreign theory.
A foreign theory was originally described in the context of ecological psychology
as abstract ideas offered by the environment that can restrict or assist possible actions.
However, this is very adjacent and can be directly used with the active inference framework
to enhance policy selection while contextualizing the environment.
We can take the concept of a foreign theory, which can replace traditional states with abstract ideas
and reducing the need for compute for inference.
And this serves as a complementary role as behavior emerges from interactions between the agent and the environment,
which bridges the abstract cognition with real-world context.
Another approach we took is the idea of scalable policy selection.
To scale policies, you need to have some sort of structure that can allow you to maintain low computation
while still scaling up the amount of policies you consider.
In our case, we use a latent suitability state in an affordance map.
What that essentially means is we have an affordance called suitability,
which encodes numerical or categorical evaluations of some region or path that the drone is about to take.
So as you can see in the diagram below, low suitability indicates high obstacle counts,
and it's not very desirable.
But high suitability means you have lower obstacle counts
and you're closer to the target location and the agent wants to be there.
This contextualization allows you to scale up policy selection because you can filter out those low suitability actions.
You don't need to consider them because they're just not suitable.
And this reduced policy set can be efficiently evaluated through EFE
without the need for some advanced hardware or off-sourcing compute.
The last area we need to consider is autonomous drone navigation.
So notoriously, autonomous drone navigation has a lot of different issues that we need to consider.
So just quickly going over some of them.
The first is that sensor data is often noisy, which makes perception and localization very tough challenges.
The next is that environmental factors like low light, glare, or clutter can additionally reduce our sensing's reliability.
After that, you have to consider that real-time adaptation to dynamic and uncertain environments is very difficult.
If you have a drone autonomously navigated in an unknown environment, it needs to be able to move and think very fast to adapt to brand new situations and issues that's never come across before.
After that, we have to think about the fact that there's limited interpretability and robustness in existing navigation algorithms.
We already talked about some of the issues with deep learning, but in the field of autonomous drone navigation, these issues are more and more important.
Then high computational load restricts deployment on low-power edge hardware.
Because in autonomous drone navigation, we're navigating environments where issues like latency are amplified.
Almost everything has to be on edge hardware, but this isn't always possible with the most frontier models.
Finally, there's often difficulty infusing multimodal data from LiDAR, camera, and IMU consistently.
We need to have all these different data sources to enable all the different elements of our navigation.
But diffuse them and provide them to the model is a challenge.
So now let's go into the actual architectural structure.
So we have a three-tiered architecture.
We have high-level inference and planning.
And these lower levels, all of this builds up and sets up the high-level planning to be as efficient and streamlined as possible.
We have our mid-level perception and encoding, which builds our world model.
It builds it from observations and its suitability and then creates a world model and beliefs about the world model, beliefs about the environment.
Finally, we have low-level control, which right now is very simple.
It just executes movement and records change in position to maintain egocentric navigation.
So let's go over these areas a bit more in-depth.
So first is perception, where we take environmental data and process it into a point cloud.
So you can see that we have the Aerosens simulation and we collect data from the LiDAR and camera, which are two primary methods of perception.
We turn these into structured observations, specifically target distance, azimuth elevation, and obstacle distance, obstacle density.
And we use these to update our world models' state base, with distance, azimuth elevation, and suitability here.
Then we take our affordances, which calculate expected suitability scores for the region around us, based on the same perception data we got from Microsoft AirSim.
Then we move to high-low planning, where we sample optimal policies from a filtered action space that's created based on our affordances.
And this high-low planning works to minimize expected free energy with its pragmatic and efficient component.
Finally, we do our final execution, setting it to a very simple low-level module for physical execution in Microsoft AirSim.
So now, let's talk about a factor graph of our architecture.
Our architecture is currently very simple, so there's not much special about our factor graph.
It's a typical message-passing structure between t and t plus 1, but there's a very key difference.
This S4, or suitability, actually feeds directly into the observation, and it dynamically weights that preference to then move into EFE.
We'll definitely talk about this more in detail once we get to the planning calculations, but this is a very key difference, and it highlights a key problem with our architecture.
Because it directly feeds in, it's actually completely deterministic, and there is no probability involved.
It's just, okay, I look at my information, I see this amount of suitability, that's my observation.
We need to make this probabilistic so that the drone doesn't have 100% trust in its sensors, because sensors are noisy, as we mentioned earlier.
Yeah, so that's definitely a feature improvement that we need to do.
Now let's go more in-depth onto the perception and world model area.
So the first thing to consider is the multimodal data acquisition.
As we mentioned earlier, our two primary methods are from the LiDAR and depth camera.
So a lot of it gives us distance data. The effective range we chose is from 0.1 to 50 meters, which is kind of arbitrary, but we found this based on testing in Microsoft AirSIM.
This is the effective range that we need for our purposes.
Then is the depth camera, which is only from 0.1 to 20 meters, and this gives us very accurate depth data.
This is an image you can see of what this some kind of sensor data would look like.
For us, what we decided to do was use a db scan algorithm to cluster all of the fuse points we get.
And we have an epsilon of 1.0 meters, which basically means that the max cluster size is 1.0 meters.
So in each area of 1.0 meters, you're going to cluster into one point and the min samples 2, meaning for one cluster, you need at least two points.
This process then abstractly represents the raw points into a discrete set of obstacles, and this internally performs denoising.
For example, if there was a random point somewhere in an individual point, then the db scan algorithm would ignore that because it's below min samples 2.
Then going more into this for preprocessing and fusion, we take our raw sensor data and filter it for some additional noise reduction and remove outer range points within the effective ranges we talked about.
And there's an optional subsampling in case computational efficiency means that we can't handle large amounts of data.
For fusion, we again just take both water data and camera data and transform it into a single unified coordinate frame,
which creates one large point cloud, which is more similar to the image you see here.
Then we do our data packaging and output for the technical part of it.
So, so far everything's been done on Python and we're calculating more information about the target, like angle, elevation, and distance.
This is mostly supplied by information from the Airstream function, which is kind of like the replacement for the ion view simulation.
Although obstacle data is also packaged here, we take the obstacle positions, which we got from db scan and the obstacle density, which is a very simple calculation that represents how dense the environment is.
And this information is then finally packaged into a JSON file and we use a ZMQ system to communicate between the Python and Julia areas of a project.
So all of this is in Python and ZMQ then sends it to Julia, which is going to go over our planning module and it's going to process it.
All right. So let's go into this actual suitability calculation.
So our suitability looks like this. We have a weight, we have safety factor, another weight, and then density factor.
The weights are just normalized to add to one. And then we have these weights here. These are hyper parameters.
There's a lot of room for improvement, which we'll talk about later.
So here's how the safety factor looks. It's an exponential where essentially, when d, the obstacle
distance, is very close to zero, you immediately know it's unsafe because it's an obstacle right in
front of me or right next to me. And our safety factor crashes as it's an exponential decay function.
So then as d approaches larger and larger numbers, it gets safer and safer and safer because the exponential
increases. And then we know, okay, I'm not close to an obstacle. Let's just ignore and let's keep going.
And then we also have this penalty, which is like, if there's an obstacle within 1.2 meters,
we replace safety or we multiply safety by an exponential negative 1 over d, which basically
says, okay, there's this obstacle that's some amount of close to me. Let's quantify that and then go from
there. So this is kind of a penalty for very unsafe environments. When there's a lot of obstacles, it
comes into play a lot. Then let's go to the density factor. It's based on an obstacle density. Both of these
are considered observations, 0.4 and 0.5, as we saw earlier. So we take the density factor, which is very
similarly structured. It's based on obstacle density. And then there's a cutoff for both. But for this
one, the cutoff is more noticeable. This is like just a density threshold that if it gets below this, it's
like it just, the density factor becomes 1 immediately, because we know, very safe. And then steepness is just
quantifies how sensitive it is to changes in density. So as density gets low, as you can probably guess,
the factor gets close to 1, so that, okay, I know it's quite safe. And then as density gets very high,
the factor gets lower to lower suitability. There's also another density penalty for high densities.
It's a bunch of stuff, but essentially it takes the maximum of a hyperparameter and then
a calculation with obstacle density and checks, okay, how much should I scale the factor to penalize this
factor so that suitability begins to lower? That eventually ends up with a normalized score
from 0 to 1, which represents this overall navigability and safety. Now you can clearly
see that there's a lot of rule-based elements that require hyperparameter sweeps. And that is a very,
very glaring problem with architecture that has a fairly arbitrary fix, which we'll definitely talk
about later. Let's go over the overall planning process. So we start with waypoint generation.
Once we have our internal suitability, we're able to do this, where we generate 25 to 75 candidate waypoints
based on obstacle density. So again, this is another example of the agent taking environmental context
and using it to inform its planning. We do a dual strategy where the sampling 70% about it is exploring
the nearby space. And there's some target bias sampling, an initial 30%, that's pushing the agent more
towards the goal. We have an adaptive radius, which is where the action length is between 1 to 10 meters,
which is again, based on internal suitability. So we use a very simple calculation where the minimum is 1,
and then you raise suitability to the power of 3, and multiply by 10 minus 1. So this basically means
as suitability approaches 0, then the action length would be approaching 1, and vice versa, suitability is
going really well towards 1, then our action length is going to be 10. There's an additional check that's
performed after this to ensure that we have at least enough to reach a target when you're in some area
that's nearby the target. So you can see from this whole process, you have flexible actions. If the
agent's in a very open space, then there's absolutely no need for it to be taking a small action. Like 1,
we'll be taking something more larger, like 7 or 8, and vice versa, if you're in very dense space,
we can take actions of when. And yeah. So let's go more into how this process works.
When we get this set of waypoints, we calculate the expected suitability. This is a very similar
calculation as Syphiky talked about, but the intro suitability, it just has a few additional
modifications and penalties. So one of them is that there's more conservative parameters used. We
pretty much use trigger cutoffs for distance and steepness, which just makes it a safer check.
We then add some additional cluster and octant penalties. So we check for any dense obstacle
clusters in any of the 8 octants around the waypoints. And if any octant is dense, which has
like 5 more than 5 obstacles, then its local density score is penalized. And if it's extremely dense,
like even more, and it has more than 10 obstacles, its local obstacle distance is also penalized.
We also have a few other penalties like corridor penalty, where the path from the current position
is checked for narrow gaps. And if the path is too narrow, then suitability is severely penalized.
This means that we're not just considering where the agent's going to end up, but the process of how
the agent's going to get there. Finally, we have this target proximity one is where, again, if the agent is
close to your target, the waypoints moving more directly towards it receive an additional suitability
boost. Yeah. And something to add is these local density score and local obstacle distance are the
same things as the factors that we talked about before. So we basically penalize or increase these
for expected suitability based on these factors. Finally, we filter. So after all the calculations,
the system discards all these waypoints with a score less than 8 to 0.8. And then you calculate
transitions for other states with a suitability of 0.95. And then we calculate EFE. So we evaluate EFE
for this action set by encoding paths with EFE based on their location and all these factors.
So our equation looks like this. We take in the current state, so distance, angle, and suitability,
and then the action, which is a 3D vector, and then the agent's current beliefs as probability
distributions. So we have the goal seeking behavior, the uncertainty reduction term, and the VFE term,
which essentially, we'll talk about later, but essentially uses VFE to penalize or increase EFE.
All right, so let's break down each part of EFE in each term. So first, the pragmatic term. It's just
a pragmatic weight. And then preference score, distance bonus, and this magnitude term. So
preference score is just your preferences for distance, angle, suitability, as a weighted average,
which we'll talk about in the next slide. The distance bonus, which is just your expected distance
minus the current target distance, and then factor of two. And finally, action magnitude. So larger
actions would incur greater scaling of this, because your pragmatic value should increase, or rather,
be minimized as you get closer to the target. So basically, in essence, a higher pragmatic value
would lead to more efficient actions, while the adaptive weighing does ensure some caveats in very
dense environments. Now let's go over the epistemic value. So just keep in mind, in general, in any
acumen-prince framework, the epistemic value is in charge of driving the agent to explore and reduce
uncertainty against beliefs. So for example, if the agent's like, for this state, I have a very low
certainty in it, what action can incentivize and help me find more information about the state? That's
what the epistemic value is encoding. So let's go over specifically in our architecture, what we have.
So the equation is very simple. We have this epistemic weight, which is by default 0.2. It's a hyperparameter.
And we do dynamically increase this in our system in high density areas, which promotes more
acceleration and looking around for safe paths. We then multiply by an additional factor of 0.5.
This is arbitrarily chosen by us. After that, we multiply by the total entropy, or which is
encoding the uncertainty of the agent's beliefs. This is computed as the equation h of q is equal to
h of the q of the location, plus h of q of our angle, plus h of q of our suitability. So in essence,
it's just calculating the total entropy of our states as per the argument's framework.
This is a more detailed equation as per the belief distribution for how the calculation works,
where you have the summation, and you take each part of the belief distribution, and add this term
epsilon to it, and take the log of that. It's very standard calculation. And finally, in this,
going back to the equation, we multiply it by the action magnitude. Again, if you remember, our actions
vary from length one to length 10. And this term just reflects that increased epistemic value for
the larger actions. You might think this is a bit strange, but logically, the motivation is,
if an agent is moving further, then it's able to uncover more information about the environment,
and thus, it has a greater epistemic value. Just note, because of the way things are set up with
the architecture, we are multiplying this by a negative, and our total EFT ends up being negative.
This is just how things are supposed to be. And overall, once we get this epistemic value,
we see that a higher epistemic value is going to incentivize the agent to reduce uncertainty about
its environment, thus balancing exploration and goal pursuit. Our final part of EFE is this BFE penalty.
This is a very non-traditional and not super necessary term, but it is still there, so we
decided to talk about it. It's a small regularization term that links our planning to the inference.
Because we don't have very explicit perceptual inference, we do this term. So lambda is just a
small fixed hyperparameter, which ensures the penalty is about 10% of EFE, so it doesn't really affect EFE
that much. And then VFE is just your variation for energy calculated during inference. It measures
how well current beliefs, explain the observation, essentially just our traditional EFE, VFE.
And we have complexity, or your entropy term, and then you have belief accuracy,
such as your expected probability of alteration over states, i.e. your likelihood. And then your prior
belief, just a simple belief updating system, which is how VFE is struggling.
Also, this all leads into control, which is just our final steps. These are simple errors and functions,
they take in all these values, most of these values are completely irrelevant, but you have x, y, z,

it in an agent based setting, but this relates to team members. So a project is defined as a set of
tasks and agents with different competencies. So you have agents that are contributors, maintainers,
project leaders. You also have tasks that are specific to contributors, specific to project leaders,
specific to maintainers, specific to other types of people, maybe technical writers,
or something like that. When we think of agents, we think of individuals. And those agents usually
have some sort of cognitive state. They also have maybe a task or an ascribed role. And agents see this
division of labor in a project, and have specific roles on a project, like contributors and maintainers.
And then tasks are defined as units of work. Sometimes we have issues, which are just kind of single tasks
that are, you know, clearly defined, very tightly defined, to what we call on our simulations,
MERS Jupiter shot projects, which are these large multi-stage projects where you have to complete one
part of the project before you know what the next part of the project will look like. So we have these tasks,
these agents, these projects, and now we're going to model them and measure them to some degree.
We're going to create metrics, but we're going to mainly focus on simulation to see what this looks
like. So again, we're going to turn to agent-based models or agent-based environments where we have,
and of course, in an agent-based model, you have this grid world. And on the grid world, you have agents
that can occupy individual cells. And those cells can be occupied by agents that have different roles,
different internal states, and they interact with each other through rules. And, you know,
if you have agents next to you, you're, you know, you can, you can share information with them,
and they create these structures across the grid that are these collectives. And so, you know,
we're going to be looking at collective behavior across the grid. We're going to be looking at
individual behavior on the grid, and we're going to see how that evolves over time.
So our agent is in a certain IJ position. It'll produce a spatial, a bunch of agents will produce
a spatial distribution with specific states. So we have a red state, a blue state, and a gray state here.

So, you know, we want to think about other kinds of processes to model, not just
agents that live on a grid world, but those agents living on a grid world do something.
So ultimately, these agents are trying to solve different collective action problems.
So, you know, there are different aspects to this, and we, but we don't want to just model
collective action for the sake of modeling collective action. We want to map this to
some process in open source. And so here we have branch development in Git, and we have pull requests.
So we have different branches where different branches are created for different purposes by
different agents, and they're all kind of pushing their work to a main branch where they're, you know,
they're kind of taking the work from the main branch, creating their own branch, finishing their work,
and then issuing a pull request back to the main branch.
So this is collective action because all the agents have to coordinate their activities so that at the
end, the main branch has like a coherent project, and it isn't just, you know, everything that's
pushed, smooshed together, and it's not workable. So, you know, there are a bunch of things that drive
collective action in this context. So the first is internal motivation. A contributor wants to work
on an issue. So if a contributor doesn't want to work on an issue, they won't bother making a branch,
and they certainly won't bother making a pull request. There also has to be cooperation. So
sometimes agents can get together, make their own branches, and use a divide and conquer strategy
to sort of address the available tasks and address what needs to be done. They coordinate their efforts
through looking at these diffs, which are the differences between different branches,
and addressing those accordingly. And then that leads to this finished project. So you have this
sort of technological layer that allows us to make these branches, compare these diffs, and do this
on an automated fashion, which helps with the cognitive load. But it's also important to have the sort of
internal motivation and this cooperative aspect of the behavior. And then, of course, there's the external
motivation, which is sort of addressing a technical layer, which is that we have to make contributing to
this project seamless and meaningful. So if the project is not, you know, easy to contribute to, and it isn't
really about anything, then people won't bother to do any of this. Another process we can model is the issue queue, or the
Kanban board. So Kanban boards are used ubiquitously in project management. Issue queues are also used to
store issues, to move issues through different types of different levels of priority from beginning to
completion. And so there are a lot of things that drive this at the individual level, at the team level,
and then sort of at the issue creation level itself. So individual prioritization can be characterized as
what issues contributors should address first, later, and last. So when we build a Kanban board,
we usually create these categories of, you know, to do, in progress, urgent, and finished. And we have to
decide as individuals where to put those issues. What's the state of completion for an issue, and where
do we put it? So, you know, maybe we don't want to work on certain issues at all. Maybe we find other issues
to be very appealing, or maybe we take all of our issues and we move very slowly through them. Maybe we
move faster. And, but we have to have this means of sort of addressing issues. Sometimes we procrastinate
on them. And, you know, we have to have this model of moving on things so that things get completed.
Then we have team prioritization. So in team prioritization, we can ask what issues are at
what stage in project development. So if we have a team reviewing a Kanban board, that team will look at
all the individuals and their collective prioritization techniques. And then they will
just basically say, okay, this is what the state of the project is. And I'm going to give feedback
to the individuals to, or we're going to give feedback to the individuals to say what they need
to do to improve these. And then issue creation is basically what is the fundamental unit of work.
So all of our individuals must have be in agreement on the same, roughly the same fundamental unit of
work, how big are your issues. And the team also has to be in agreement or else you have a lot of

a class project, which is motivation, but it's not the same as being like kind of motivated by the problem.

multi-arm bandit comes from when people pull the lever of a slot machine. So you're like priming
this stochastic process. And you'll have like in front of you, if you've ever gone to a casino,
you'll see people sometimes put like five slot machines at once, and they'll pull the arms down
and then see what they get. That's what you're doing in a multi-arm bandit. And you're trying to
figure out whether you should explore a space or exploit a space. So if you think about that in
terms of issues, should you explore an issue deeply or should you explore more issues in a more shallow
way? We implement this in different ways so that we can look at that as a sort of a factor for driving
open source project behavior. The second point is building a statistical description of the mind. So
this is of course is the internal components or the internal model of each agent. So we want to build
a statistical description of that mind. We want to give it beliefs and we want to have, you know,
have each agent minimize its own free energy. And so this is something that of course, we also want
the agents to sort of be able to figure out what other agents are thinking to so that they can match
their beliefs and minimize their free energy together. So this is kind of where we're going with this
in terms of the collective part of it. And then of course, when we reduce our error,
it reinforces causal relationships that are necessary to complete the project. So getting the agents to see
what the causal relationships are between different parts of the project. If we can reduce their
inferential error, then we can get all the agents to move in the same direction.
So this was implemented using an inter-inforactively. So this is the link to interactively. It was used
to do two things. Number one is to implement decision problems in a single agent, as we said. So each of
these agents have some decision process going on inside of them. And then two is to implement a theory
of mind to infer the state of neighboring agents. So the agent on the left is trying to characterize the
state of the agent on the right, because the agent on the left doesn't know what the agent on the right
is thinking. And if they're both thinking the same thing, they're able to maximize utility in the same
way, then they'll be able to cooperate better and do things like divide and conquer. So this is where you
have this agent is asking this other agent, is this what you're thinking? And they say, yes.

that's a little bit harder to determine. So let's walk through some of these methods
for reinforcement learning to see maybe how they compare with active inference. So we start with Q-learning.
So for any Markov decision process, we identify optimal action selection policy. So what we want
to do with that action selection policy is maximize the expected value of the total reward.
Okay. So then this determines the quality of a given policy. An action A sub T with respect to state S
of T provided a learning rate of alpha. So we have this equation down here where we
have these different components. We have alpha times Q, where we're evaluating the action and the state,
the quality of each action and state that evaluates the current action selection value.
We add that to this estimate of optimal future values, where the state of highest quality,
and so we're looking for the maximum quality. And then that's our kind of the way, the direction
we're going in Q-learning. We're trying to find that maximum feature value, the maximum quality
of a given set of policies. Now, the multi-arm bandit is also used then in reinforcement learning,
like we saw with active inference. It's used for task allocation, role matching given prior performance.
It encourages specialization while allowing for flexibility. And it utilizes what we call on-policy
temporal difference learning, which we'll get into next. And so it's important to recall the different
community member types, maintainer, contributor, innovator, knowledge curator. And we can use this
to think about how to allocate different tasks to these different classes of a contributor,
or these different classes of project member. So temporal difference learning can be stated as a state
value function of a finite state Markov decision process under a policy pi. V sub pi is equal to the
state value function of the Markov decision process with states, rewards, and a discount rate,
importance, which states the importance of future rewards. So we have, instead of our learning rate,
we have a discount rate. And then we estimate V pi s, which is the function for the state value. And we
evaluate this policy pi, we obtain the reward r, and we update our value function. Now, some of the metrics
that were created in the reinforcement learning work, and this was done by VD Ruhira, she developed five
different metrics that work in this context. So the first is the harmony index. So remember, I said there
are different things we want to measure. And then there are different things that we can sort of have
as like an output. So the first the harmony index, this captures workload balance and tasks success rate.
This is basically measures how well balanced workflow workloads are, how much cognitive load there is
for each agent, and then the success of agents on those tasks. Then there's the resilience quotient,
which measures adaptability during disruptions, such as dropouts and workload spikes. It means basically
how resilient are you to different types of, you know, disruptions in your work in the project.
There's reassignment overhead, which tracks inefficiency caused by reassigning tasks.
There's an initial, an initial density of agents. So basically, it's where if you have a dense setting
of agents to start with, it's going to give you a very different result than a very, very small number
of agents. And we can use that to sort of normalize a lot of the heterogeneity in open source projects.
And then the contributor maintainer vision. So if we don't have a good vision, agents won't know if
there's anything for them to do. But if the vision is there, then at least they have in their mind
sort of a mental model that they have something to do. And so they go to find specific tasks to do,
to solve specific issues. The next thing I want to talk about is how large language models were used.

technical skills. So Linus Torvalds is the benevolent dictator in the Linux example. And then the

they can in the project. I want to get into the way we're thinking about integrating a lot of these

interesting thing, because none of those are described by any of the models that we've talked about.
So this is a point for future work. We also know that like some of these dynamics, because you get
a lot of people leaving a lot of people joining, a lot of flux and churn, that it can create vulnerabilities,
as well as opportunities. And so if we want to know how to sort of build a model that can help us
understand maybe what those opportunities are, those vulnerabilities are, there are some ways forward.
So one way is to build these sort of what we call meta brain models, where you have different,
you take different models, and you put them together, different layers of different types
of representations, put them together as a structure that sort of, you know, communicates selectively

working together on tasks that are semantically defined, and then maybe an agent based model, and
sort of the driver of collective behavior. So you have this agent based model, sort of coordinating all
these different brains, composed of large language models, multi agent reinforcement learning, active
inference models.
So I want to finish with sort of a thought from Jesse Perrin, and he's another person in our lab who's
contributed to this work. And he's been thinking a lot about project management, and a lot about how
we often create tools that don't go anywhere, or we have like initiatives that kind of die, because no one finds them relevant.
So he says sustainability translates to problems in academic communities. Sometimes if you go to an academic
community, you find that someone earns like a grant for tool building, so they get funding for tool building,
they build these great tools, and then the funding runs out. And so they have this open source tool that
they put out in the world, the funding runs out, and it becomes an unmaintained tool, eventually to die
a premature death. So that's one path that's suboptimal, I think, from the standpoint of open source
sustainability, where you get money to build something, you build it, and then it dies.
The other example that he gives is that someone builds a tool, and because it's not really relevant to
anything, the tool is ignored and not maintained. And so then that's bad, but it gets worse because then
someone else builds a similar tool, and that dies because it's ignored and not maintained. So you end
up with this graveyard of tools that are all maybe pretty decent, but because no one's finding them
relevant, and no one's putting the money into maintenance, the people keep reinventing the wheel,
and then the wheel breaks apart. So that's not good. So, you know, Jesse makes this observation
that academic communities more generally will often build out infrastructure, which goes nowhere,
and is disconnected from real world impact. So that's something to think about if we're thinking
about the Active Inference Institute, or our own lab, or other institutions where people build
infrastructure, it goes nowhere, or it's not connected to the real world in relevant ways. And sometimes
you just can't, you know, you can't avoid it, because you don't necessarily know what people will find
relevant. But that's part of sustainability that we haven't addressed, and maybe is sort of the secret
sauce in this whole equation. So thank you for your attention.
